## [Reproducible Evaluation Harness Enables Safe Prompt Compression in Production Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31972) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an offline evaluation harness for a production conversational agent that remains reproducible despite live data changes and can be regenerated on demand. Using this harness as the fitness function for GEPA-based prompt optimization under a correctness gate, it reduces the agent's full prompt surface by 3.5-7.6% without regressing held-out task correctness across three independent subject-model families. This work addresses a key challenge in deploying conversational agents on mutable enterprise data: evaluation drift. By providing a reproducible evaluation method and demonstrating safe prompt compression, it enables more efficient and reliable production agents, potentially reducing costs and improving maintainability. The compression is guided by a correctness gate to prevent reward hacking, and the approach is validated across three independent subject-model families, with one showing measurable correctness improvement. The harness can be regenerated when stale, ensuring long-term reproducibility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Evaluating conversational agents that operate on live enterprise data is difficult because backend state changes cause replayed sessions to drift, making benchmarks stale. GEPA (Genetic-Pareto) is a prompt optimizer that uses natural language reflection and Pareto-aware selection to evolve high-performing prompt variants with minimal evaluations. Prompt compression aims to reduce prompt size and cost but risks degrading quality if not carefully validated.

## [RSI-Opt: Recursive Self-Improvement Optimizer Agents for Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32026) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RSI-Opt introduces a plug-and-play recursive self-improvement meta-agent framework that evolves prompts, playbooks, optimizer code, and decision cadence to improve LLM-driven training optimization across generations. Across 5 generations on three training tasks, the meta-agents develop reusable procedures and code, with representative raw-space summary z-scores increasing from +10.48 to +14.33 on the evolution suite with Opus 5.5 and from -0.36 to +3.11 on held-out tasks with GPT-5.5 through cross-model policy transfer. This work shows how LLM agents can accumulate reusable optimization knowledge and executable tools across training runs, moving beyond fixed-policy optimizer tuning toward self-improving AutoML. It could impact automated hyperparameter and optimizer tuning, scientific machine learning, and the design of agentic systems that learn from experience. On the Burgers PDE, RSI-Opt reduces mean relative L2 error by approximately 2.7-fold with Opus 5.5 and 8.8-fold with GPT-6.1 over the fixed-prompt agent baseline, though PDE transfer varies across LLMs and tasks. Controlled comparisons indicate that initial decisions capture most gains on seen tasks, while continued adaptation improves outcomes on unseen tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement refers to a process where an AI system iteratively improves its own capabilities, often by rewriting its own code or policies. In neural network training, optimizers and hyperparameters are typically chosen via fixed recipes or manual tuning; LLM agents have recently been used to monitor training and adjust settings, but fixed agent policies do not accumulate experience across runs. RSI-Opt addresses this by using a meta-agent to evolve the agent's policy—including prompts, procedures, code, and decision cadence—based on evidence from earlier runs, with an archive of evaluated policies.

## [Test-Time Memory Evolution Outperforms Test-Time Training for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32046) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Test-Time Memory Evolution (TTME), a plug-and-play test-time adaptation method that updates external memory instead of model weights, and shows it outperforms test-time training (TTT) on all five mathematical tasks while reducing FLOPs by 82.8–84.6% and runtime by 47.7–58.4%. This work offers a computationally efficient alternative to TTT, which requires costly online weight updates, making it more feasible to scale test-time adaptation to larger models for scientific discovery. It could influence future research on efficient test-time adaptation and memory-based methods for LLMs. TTME continually updates external memory by distilling experience to guide subsequent discovery without updating model weights, and the comparison with TTT was conducted under the same discovery framework, replacing only the test-time adaptation method. The evaluation is limited to five mathematical tasks, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time training (TTT) adapts a pretrained model during inference by updating its weights on the test data, which can improve performance on scientific discovery tasks but incurs additional computational cost for online weight updates. Test-time adaptation more broadly refers to adapting a model at inference time using only test data, and external memory is an alternative way to persist and reuse experience without changing model parameters.

## [HyScale Accelerates Long-Context Training for Learned Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32057) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HyScale is a new long-context training system that computes exact top-k selection on the fly without materializing a full score table and replays selection indices during recomputation, achieving a 2.07× speedup over Megatron-LM on 1M-token sequences with 512 H20 GPUs. Learned sparse attention is already used in production models like DeepSeek-V3.2, and selection has become a training bottleneck; HyScale's systems-level optimizations could significantly reduce the cost of training million-token context models. HyScale introduces HyTopK, which finds each query's exact top-k without a score table, and selection replay, which reuses forward-pass selection during recomputation; it also speeds up CSA layers of DeepSeek-V4-Flash and QSA layers of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next by 2.01× and 1.57× at 1M tokens on 32 H800 GPUs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Learned sparse attention is an architecture for handling million-token contexts where each query attends to only a small set of previous tokens selected by a learned scoring function. During training, activation checkpointing is often used to save memory by recomputing activations in the backward pass, but for sparse attention, the selection step must be repeated, adding overhead. Megatron-LM is a widely used framework for large-scale distributed training, and HyScale builds on it to optimize this selection bottleneck.

## [DriveMind: Particle-Filter-Inspired Belief Tracking Boosts MLLM Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32069) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DriveMind introduces a belief-tracking framework for MLLM-based autonomous driving that maintains multiple scene hypotheses and updates their weights via particle-filter-inspired propagation and resampling. On Bench2Drive, it improves Driving Score from 68.50 to 81.37 and Success Rate from 41.36% to 54.09%. This addresses a key limitation of current MLLM-based drivers that reason from scratch each frame and commit to a single interpretation, which can lead to errors in ambiguous scenes. The significant gains, especially on unsignalized intersections and oncoming-traffic interactions, suggest belief tracking could make autonomous driving more robust and safe. DriveMind propagates each hypothesis through a vision-language model, scores it against the current observation, and resamples to maintain diversity. The largest gains occur on ambiguous scenarios such as unsignalized intersections (+20.0 DS) and oncoming-traffic interactions (+20.5 DS), and it also improves merging, emergency braking, and give-way abilities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process images and text together, enabling them to perceive traffic scenes and propose driving actions within a single model. Particle filtering is a sequential Monte Carlo method that represents a probability distribution using a set of weighted samples (particles), which are propagated and resampled over time to track multiple hypotheses. Bench2Drive is a benchmark for evaluating autonomous driving systems in closed-loop settings, measuring metrics like Driving Score (DS) and Success Rate (SR).

## [DELA: Plug-in Adapter Enables Controllable Draft Edits for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32096) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DELA introduces a plug-in framework that enables explicit, controllable draft-level editing (Insert, Delete, Remask, Stop) for frozen diffusion language models, separating high-level edit control from token-level denoising. It uses a two-phase decoding procedure and a staged curriculum to train the controller, achieving gains of up to 5.5 percentage points over direct diffusion decoding on mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks. This work addresses a key limitation of diffusion language models—implicit refinement via token-level masking schedules—by giving users explicit control over when and where to edit drafts. It could make DLMs more practical for tasks requiring precise, iterative text editing while keeping the backbone frozen, lowering adaptation costs. The two-phase decoding first uses a fast, append-only drafting phase that supports KV-cache reuse, followed by a global refinement phase enabling targeted non-monotonic edits to the full draft. The controller is trained with a staged curriculum: learning local edit priors from synthetic recovery tasks, then refining edit selection via rollout-based outcome preferences and downstream task feedback.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion language models (DLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising a whole sequence in parallel, unlike autoregressive models that predict tokens left-to-right. In masked diffusion, generation is controlled by token-level masking schedules that decide which positions to unmask or remask, but draft-level refinement remains implicit. KV-cache reuse is a common inference optimization that stores key/value tensors for previously processed tokens, but arbitrary interior edits can disrupt prefix stability and limit its effectiveness.

Researchers introduced PersonaScienceBench, a new benchmark of 200 tasks drawn from five existing benchmarks across four scientific domains, where simulated researchers with distinct persona profiles provide context and feedback through conversation while task objectives and grading stay fixed. The benchmark was tested across eighteen model configurations, and detailed analyses using GPT 6 Astra showed that agents exhibit distinct interaction behaviors—such as targeted clarification and workflow revision—even when final scores are identical. Evaluation covers task performance, fact recovery, and self-repetition, and interaction traces reveal behaviors like targeted clarification and workflow revision that can differ even when final scores match.

## [Warped Tool Output Formatters Mislead LLM Agents and Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32110) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.32110) demonstrates that tool output formatters which omit details such as search limits, result caps, or identifier namespaces can cause LLM agents and verifiers to make unsupported claims, even when the underlying tool computation is correct. In a production RAG system used by the Pilsudski Institute in New York, repairing the formatter wording substantially reduced unsupported agent claims for three of four failure types, and in one test verifiers went from accepting incorrect answers 90 out of 90 times to rejecting them 90 out of 90 times. This work highlights a subtle but practical reliability risk for anyone building agentic systems: the text a tool prints can misrepresent what the tool actually did, and verifiers that rely on that text may fail to catch errors. The finding that a simple wording fix can dramatically improve verification accuracy suggests that output formatting deserves attention as a first-class design concern in LLM agent pipelines. The study used two formatter versions—one with key omissions and one with all missing details clearly stated—and found that the difference alone changed what agents claimed and what verifiers accepted. The authors also provide an audit prompt used with Codex and Claude Code; when applied to an old version of their own system it rediscovered live defects and surfaced twelve more, and when applied to OpenClaw and Hermes it found warped formatters in both, with the OpenClaw fix already merged.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems enhance large language models by retrieving external information before generating answers, and LLM agents often rely on tools to perform searches or computations. Verifiers are secondary models or checks that judge whether an agent's output is correct. This paper shows that the text interface between tools and agents—the formatter—can introduce misleading omissions that neither agents nor verifiers are equipped to detect without explicit facts.

## [MPQ: A Message-Passing View of Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32127) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MPQ reframes row-wise post-training quantization as posterior inference over discrete codebooks and introduces a solver that uses vector approximate message passing followed by block coordinate descent to achieve better low-bit quantization than existing methods like GPTQ. This work provides a novel theoretical framing that could lead to more accurate 2-bit and 3-bit quantization, enabling efficient deployment of large language models with reduced memory and compute requirements. MPQ preserves soft posterior information across blocks during inference and commits to hard codewords only at the output stage; it improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy on Llama, Qwen, and Mistral models at 2-bit lattice and 3/4-bit scalar quantization, and composes with activation-aware scaling and incoherence processing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses the weights of a trained large language model into low-bit representations without retraining, reducing memory and speeding up inference. GPTQ is a widely used PTQ method that quantizes weights one block at a time, committing each block to a single codeword before considering its neighbors. Vector approximate message passing is an iterative algorithm from statistical physics for solving high-dimensional inference problems, and block coordinate descent is an optimization technique that updates one block of variables at a time.

<a id="item-60"></a>
## [DIVA: Decision-Theoretic Information Seeking for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32181) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose DIVA (Decision-Informed Value-of-information Acquisition), a training-free procedure that lets an LLM specify a small decision model and value each candidate check by its expected improvement in decision outcomes net of cost, stopping when no check is worth it. A variant, DIVA-U, accounts for the LLM's uncertainty in estimating how strongly each check shifts the probability of a bad outcome. LLMs often over-query or pay for evidence that cannot change their decision, wasting cost and time; DIVA addresses this by grounding information seeking in value-of-information, which could make agentic LLM systems more efficient and reliable. Across four domains and 20 LLMs, DIVA-U achieved lower regret than every LLM baseline on real-world data, and DIVA cut the best baseline's regret by 75% in synthetic scenarios. DIVA is training-free and applies to any LLM, requiring the model to answer short numeric questions to build a decision model; DIVA-U explicitly models the LLM's uncertain estimates of check reliability. The approach was evaluated across four domains and 20 LLMs, with code available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Value of information (VoI) is a decision-theoretic metric that quantifies the expected gain in decision performance from acquiring additional information, net of its cost. The expected value of sample information (EVSI) is the expected increase in utility from sampling additional observations before making a decision. LLMs used as decision-support agents often struggle with knowing when to stop gathering costly evidence, a problem DIVA aims to solve by applying VoI principles.

## [Learned Foresight and Balanced Bands Improve Watermarking for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32205) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces two learned components—vocabulary bands that serve as green lists and a foresight predictor that estimates how each candidate token shifts its neighbors' predictions—to improve red-green watermarking for diffusion language models. On WaterBench with LLaDA-8B-Instruct, each learned component raises the detection z-score at equal distortion, and their gains are nearly additive, detecting at least 96% of responses at a 1% false-positive rate while matching the LLM-judge score of unwatermarked text. Watermarking is a key tool for tracing AI-generated text, but existing red-green schemes assume autoregressive left-to-right generation and break down for diffusion language models that choose tokens out of order. This work closes that gap with learned components and exact null distributions, making watermark detection more reliable for an emerging class of non-autoregressive models. The method counts green tokens once per distinct context-token pair so repetition earns nothing, and the learned vocabulary bands separate competing candidates while keeping the null distribution exact. Experiments also show more detected responses on Dream and as many or more on code and question answering without significant task-accuracy loss.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Red-green watermarking embeds a secret signal by favoring tokens from a 'green list' derived from a secret key and preceding context, which a detector can later verify statistically. Diffusion language models generate text by iteratively refining tokens in arbitrary order rather than strictly left to right, so a token may be chosen before its predecessors and later influence their green lists, complicating standard watermarking. LLaDA-8B-Instruct is an 8-billion-parameter diffusion language model used here as the main testbed.

## [RxnExBench: New Benchmark for Chemical Reaction Extraction from Literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32221) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RxnExBench introduces a multimodal benchmark with 3,565 expert-annotated reactions and 2,361 molecular structures from high-impact chemistry journals, using a unified schema of 15 reaction roles and 26 core fields. Evaluation of 9 state-of-the-art vision-language models shows substantial performance degradation as schema complexity and reasoning demands increase. This benchmark addresses the challenge of automatically extracting structured reaction data from information-dense chemistry figures, which is crucial for AI-for-Chemistry tasks like retrosynthetic planning and yield prediction. It provides a standardized evaluation resource that could drive improvements in chemistry-aware multimodal understanding. The benchmark includes a reasoning-driven difficulty taxonomy, a modular annotation pipeline, and the Oracle-MolRec protocol that separates molecular recognition from higher-level reasoning. Results under Oracle-MolRec reveal that accurate molecular recognition alone is insufficient, with multimodal integration and chemistry-aware reasoning remaining primary bottlenecks.

**Background**: Screen Tables and Substrate Scopes are common figures in synthetic chemistry literature that summarize reaction conditions and outcomes in a dense tabular format. AI-for-Chemistry aims to automate tasks like retrosynthetic planning, where a target molecule is broken down into simpler precursors, and yield prediction, both of which rely on high-quality reaction data.

## [SolveBind helps GUI agents pass CAPTCHA verification barriers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32223) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SolveBind, a GUI-agent framework that combines a hierarchical solving tree (Perceive, Ground, Solve, Act, Verify) with a runtime supervisor to convert correct CAPTCHA solutions into accepted interactions. Across nine CAPTCHA task families and nine backbone models, SolveBind achieved the highest success rate on eight backbones, outperforming the strongest baseline by 13.3–34.5 percentage points, and on all six AndroidWorld backbones. This work addresses an underexplored but practical bottleneck: agents that can solve verification puzzles still fail because anti-automation defenses judge behavioral signals like timing and interaction trajectories, not just outcomes. The results suggest that evidence-grounded execution control can significantly improve end-to-end task completion in protected GUI environments, affecting GUI-agent developers and evaluation benchmarks. Ablation studies show that removing binding drops success from 82.22% to 71.56% and increases latency and token usage, while removing recovery caps raises success slightly to 83.56% but inflates latency and token usage to 3.7x and 2.4x. The authors also note failure boundaries of heuristic submission rules and a success-cost trade-off in recovery.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: GUI agents are AI systems that operate graphical user interfaces by perceiving screenshots and issuing clicks or keystrokes. CAPTCHAs and anti-automation systems are designed to distinguish humans from bots by analyzing behavioral signals such as mouse trajectories, timing, and interaction patterns, so simply producing a correct answer is often not enough. SolveBind introduces runtime supervision that grounds action commitments in tool-derived evidence and mediates execution under explicit recovery budgets to handle these dynamic checks.

## [MDL Compression Ratio Separates Law-Grade from Statistical Knowledge in Self-Improving Science Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32245) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A self-improving science agent that had admitted 252 relations through held-out prediction courts used the Minimum Description Length (MDL) compression ratio, originally for model selection, as an unsupervised epistemic grader. The compression-ranked top band exactly recovered canonical laws (vis-viva across seven planets, Balmer/Rydberg) with enrichment p=3.6e-12, while retroactively revealing that 86% of its ledger compresses nothing (cr≈1), including a predictor with held-out R²=0.541 that was statistical noise. This is the first measured instance of a discovery agent auditing its own knowledge ledger by an internal measure and finding itself over-claiming, showing that prediction accuracy (R²) and compression value (cr) are nearly decoupled axes of knowledge. It offers a scalable unsupervised vetting method for AI-generated scientific laws, which is critical as generation outpaces human review. The method formalizes dual-axis bookkeeping of prediction value (R²) and compression value (cr), demonstrates robustness to quantization, and registers forward falsifiable predictions. The enrichment p-value comes from a hypergeometric test, and the top band shows physically coherent band structure with zero counterexamples.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: The Minimum Description Length (MDL) principle is a model selection method that favors models enabling the most compact representation of data plus the model itself. In scientific discovery, AI agents now generate candidate laws at scale, making vetting the bottleneck. The hypergeometric test is a statistical method for enrichment analysis, often used to assess over-representation of certain categories.

## [RSIHub: A Composable Framework for Agent-Evolution Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32253) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RSIHub is an open-source framework that unifies the implementation of agent-evolution methods through reusable operators and recipes, demonstrated by instantiating AHE, Hyperagents, A-Evolve, and GEPA without modifying the framework core. Evaluated with MiniSWE and Codex on Terminal Bench 2 and τ³ Banking, it reports a maximum overall pass@1 gain of 13.5 percentage points. This framework addresses the fragmentation in agent-evolution research by providing shared infrastructure for execution, evaluation, recovery, and lineage, allowing researchers to focus on evolution policies rather than rebuilding experimental machinery. It enables fair comparisons and reproducible results, which could accelerate progress in developing more capable LLM agents. The evaluation shows that method rankings vary across settings and that optimization gains do not always transfer to held-out tasks, highlighting the importance of robust evaluation. RSIHub keeps evaluation outside the mutable surface and ties results to candidates and evaluation conditions, ensuring inspectable candidate histories.

**Background**: Agent-evolution methods use execution feedback to iteratively improve prompts, skills, tools, and orchestration of LLM agents. Examples include AHE, Hyperagents, A-Evolve, and GEPA, with GEPA being a reflective prompt evolution technique that outperforms reinforcement learning in some settings. However, these methods often rebuild similar experimental infrastructure, making comparison difficult. RSIHub provides a common protocol to standardize such comparisons.

## [MedCaseAgent Turns Multimodal Clinical Notes into Rare Disease Case Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32258) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present MedCaseAgent, an end-to-end agentic reasoning framework that synthesizes publication-quality rare disease case reports from unstructured multimodal clinical notes by combining patient-level data mining with iterative, tool-integrated reasoning. The work is accompanied by the release of MedCase-150K, a large-scale multimodal clinical case-report corpus, MedCase-Bench, a temporally held-out evaluation suite, and an open-source model post-trained from Qwen3.5-9B and optimized for this task. Rare and atypical disease case reports demand rigorous, evidence-based reasoning that general-purpose LLMs often fail to deliver, so an agent that dynamically queries medical literature and knowledge bases while assessing case novelty could help clinicians document and share unusual cases more reliably. The modular, plug-and-play design and the released corpus and benchmark also give the medical AI community reusable infrastructure for evaluating evidence-grounded clinical generation. MedCaseAgent uses a hierarchical structure that integrates patient-level data mining with iterative tool-integrated reasoning, and it supports various backbone LLMs in a plug-and-play fashion. Under both LLM-judged and rule-based evaluation it outperforms existing general-purpose and specialized medical LLMs and agents, with blinded expert reviews and ablations further supporting its clinical relevance and evidence grounding; however, the work is an arXiv preprint that has not undergone peer review.

## [TraceTrial-Bench: New Benchmark Evaluates AI Agent Harness Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32285) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TraceTrial-Bench introduces a benchmark with 95 expert-level tasks and a trajectory tracing module to evaluate AI agent harness reliability across seven components, separating agent performance from harness quality. The benchmark includes a two-layer evaluation framework and a Failure Attribution module, and was tested across 4 harnesses, 9 models, and 95 tasks, yielding 3,420 runs with macro-averaged harness scores from 59.8% to 72.0%. Most existing benchmarks focus on model capabilities rather than the harness that turns an LLM into an agent, so this work fills a gap by providing an auditable and reproducible way to compare harnesses and diagnose their failures. It could help developers choose better scaffolding and understand how harness design interacts with model behavior, especially since 31.1% of runs succeeded despite failing at least one harness criterion. The benchmark organizes harness reliability into seven components: context, execution, governance, lifecycle, observability, tools, and verification. It uses a Multi-source Evidence Acquisition module to gather evidence from agent context, harness, and execution environment for LLM-based evaluation, and a Failure Attribution module with explicit mechanism chains for run-level attribution.

**Background**: An agent harness, also known as agent scaffolding, is the software infrastructure surrounding a large language model that enables it to operate as an AI agent, handling tasks like tool use, memory, and execution control. As LLM agents move from conversation to long-running work in repositories, browsers, and terminals, task success depends on both the model and the harness. Failure attribution is the process of tracing a failure back to the responsible agent or step, which is important for debugging multi-component systems.

## [MemCryst: Bidirectional Memory-Skill Learning for Lifelong LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32295) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MemCryst introduces a bidirectional memory-skill learning framework that enables lifelong context adaptation for LLM agents while keeping the underlying language model frozen. Recurring episodes in memory drive skill creation and revision, and skill-guided interactions generate new memory, with task outcomes adjusting retrieval influence without rewriting stored content. This addresses a core bottleneck for long-running LLM agents: operating within a bounded context window while continually learning from expanding interaction history. By coupling memory and skills bidirectionally with a frozen model, the approach offers a practical path to lifelong adaptation without costly model retraining, relevant to embodied control, long-document reading, conversation, math, and code tasks. Across ten settings spanning embodied control, long-document reading, conversation, mathematics, and code, MemCryst leads seven reported comparisons; on ALFWorld, joint memory-skill serving achieves 63.17% success versus 59.83% for memory-only and 50.50% for skill-only. The framework adjusts the future retrieval influence of served records based on task outcomes without rewriting their content, and code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems that use a large language model as their core reasoning engine, augmented with external components such as planning and memory to perform tasks autonomously. Because a model's context window is bounded, long-running agents cannot simply keep all past interactions in context; they must retrieve relevant memories and reusable skills. Memory provides past experience, while skills provide reusable procedures, but each alone may be insufficient for new tasks. MemCryst explores learning both jointly while keeping the language model frozen, avoiding parameter updates.

## [DMAIC-Bench tests LLM agents' ability to stop when evidence warrants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32305) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DMAIC-Bench introduces 97 seeded Lean Six Sigma tasks across 15 industrial cases, using paired counterfactual variants that require opposite actions at the same workflow position to test whether agents can appropriately stop or redirect. Across 13 models from six providers, execution-level routing accuracy averaged 92% on matched GO decisions but only 19% on NO-GO decisions, and blinded expert review estimated that reports act on invalid evidence at 47% of reached NO-GO decisions. This work identifies and measures a previously untested agent capability—self-restraint—showing that high task completion can mask a failure to stop when intermediate evidence disallows further analysis. It matters for agent evaluation research and for deploying LLM agents in high-stakes analytical workflows where proceeding on invalid evidence can be costly. The benchmark counts any forbidden downstream computation as a route error, and execution traces show agents reporting correct diagnostics before performing analyses those results disallow; explicit stopping permission helps some models but does not consistently restore correct decisions at both GO and NO-GO forks. Sixteen life-sciences tasks in bulk RNA-seq and case-control GWAS demonstrate the framework extends beyond industrial workflows, and code and data are available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Lean Six Sigma's DMAIC (Define, Measure, Analyze, Improve, Control) methodology imposes explicit statistical prerequisites that determine whether an analysis step is valid, making it a natural testbed for evidence-dependent decision-making. Self-reflection, where an agent critiques its own output, is widely studied in LLM agents, but a critique does not show whether evidence actually changed the next action; DMAIC-Bench calls the missing capability self-restraint. Counterfactual paired variants—tasks that are identical except for a seeded change that flips the correct action—allow the benchmark to distinguish warranted restraint from indiscriminate stopping.

Researchers introduced Solvent, an 8B large language model trained via a two-round post-training curriculum to perform long-horizon multi-step retrosynthesis planning with iterative verification and route revision. After a 2.1x performance boost from reinforcement learning, Solvent scores 61.86% across four benchmarks, surpassing mid-scale language models and prior search-based systems while rivaling trillion-parameter LLMs. This work demonstrates that long-horizon reasoning, previously proven in domains like AI-driven mathematics, can be effectively extended to complex chemical synthesis planning, potentially accelerating drug discovery and materials design. Solvent interleaves iterative synthesis planning, external availability verification, and adaptive route revision, and its 2.1x improvement comes specifically from reinforcement learning. The 61.86% score is an average across four benchmarks, and the model is compared against both mid-scale LLMs and prior search-based retrosynthesis systems.

## [Cross-model validation measures LLM agent skill internalization for retirement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32322) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.32322) proposes a cross-model validation method to measure whether an LLM agent has internalized a skill, addressing the flaw in gain-based retirement rules that conflate internalized skills with uncovered capabilities. The authors release 1,251 skills with tasks and item scores under four models. This work matters because public skill hubs with up to 100,000 entries need reliable ways to retire skills that a model has already internalized, and the proposed method avoids removing skills that are merely uncovered. It has practical implications for skill hub curation and for evaluating agent capabilities across models. The method withholds the skill, scores the model on a task synthesized from it, and trusts a high unaided score only when another model gains from the skill, since nearly half of high scores come from tasks no model benefits from. On SkillsBench, 28 of 31 retirements rest on probes no run passed in full, and unaided verdicts reproduce across tasks more often than the gain rule's (Cohen's kappa 0.42 vs 0.23) but differ across models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents retrieve skills from public hubs that hold up to about 100,000 entries and serve many models. As models improve, a hub must find the skills a given model has internalized—meaning it can do without them—and retire them for that model, yet internalization has not been measured with known reliability. Published rules that compare runs with and without a skill retire on the gain alone, but the gain is near zero both for an internalized skill and when neither the model nor the skill delivers the capability, a state called uncovered. SkillsBench is a benchmark that evaluates how well agent skills work across diverse tasks.

Tencent researchers introduced TGR-EvoDAG, a multi-agent code evolution framework that models the evolution of generative recommendation pipelines as a directed acyclic graph (DAG) to maintain multiple branches and merge complementary innovations. On public Amazon datasets, it achieved up to 37.4% improvement in offline metrics and 19.7% higher Agent4Rec simulated user satisfaction over the strongest baseline, and in deployment on Tencent's large-scale commercial media platform it delivered a 12.16% relative improvement in offline Hit@1 and a statistically significant 0.40% increase in online CTR. This work addresses two practical problems in LLM-driven algorithm discovery for recommender systems—cost blindness to asymmetric retraining overheads and short-sighted evaluation that discards temporarily regressing upstream innovations—and demonstrates real industrial impact with an online CTR gain on a major platform. It shows that domain-aware, DAG-structured multi-agent evolution can outperform generic or linear frameworks for complex, multi-stage generative recommendation pipelines. TGR-EvoDAG introduces stage-aware cost planning to optimize GPU-hour budgets and active downstream co-adaptation to navigate temporary regressions and unlock cross-stage gains. The framework unifies advanced search with rich diagnostic feedback specific to generative recommendation, and the reported results include both offline evaluations on Amazon datasets and an online A/B test on Tencent's commercial media platform.

Evaluated on 142 SEIS/EIS investments with resolved outcomes, the full pipeline achieved a Spearman ρ of 0.45 with total-value multiple and AUC 0.71 for identifying write-offs, compared to ρ=0.38 and AUC 0.68 for an unconstrained LLM. This work addresses a critical challenge in applying LLMs to high-stakes decision support: score inflation and run-to-run variance that undermine trust and auditability. The evidence-gated approach and empirical validation on real investment outcomes offer a practical blueprint for practitioners in venture capital and other domains where LLM-generated scores inform consequential decisions. The system implements three mechanisms: a stage-aware corroboration ceiling (78, 70, or 60 out of 100) on factual categories lacking external evidence, a sceptic call that can only lower scores, and a pre-fetch pattern that retrieves all evidence before generation. In a study of 20 inbound applications, analyst time per application fell from 44.7 to 18.9 minutes, but 70% of reports needed at least one correction and retrieval was not date-restricted, potentially overstating screening skill.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: SEIS (Seed Enterprise Investment Scheme) and EIS (Enterprise Investment Scheme) are UK government venture capital schemes that offer tax incentives to investors in early-stage companies, making them a common structure for UK startup fundraising. Large language models can process startup pitch decks, but directly asking them for investment scores often leads to inflated ratings, inconsistent outputs across runs, and rationales that are difficult to audit. Spearman's rank correlation coefficient (ρ) measures how well two rankings agree, and AUC (area under the ROC curve) measures how well a model distinguishes between outcomes such as write-offs versus successes.

## [LeafSkill: External Skill Reuse Layer for Recursive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32412) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LeafSkill introduces an external reuse layer that retrieves and binds leaf-level action grounding experience for recursive LLM agents, using a lightweight 1.7B selector to avoid redundant large-model calls on atomic subtasks. On 200 Robotouille tasks, it completes 137 tasks versus 121 for ReCAP (68.5% vs. 60.5%, McNemar p=.0259), and on commonly completed tasks reduces Qwen3.5-27B token usage by 14.42% and calls by 14.08%. This work targets a concrete inefficiency in recursive LLM agents: repeatedly invoking large models for familiar atomic actions. By keeping skills outside model parameters and using a frozen lightweight selector, it can improve task completion while reducing host-model inference without replacing high-level planning, which could lower costs and latency for long-horizon agent deployments. Under lexical and syntactic paraphrases, exact matching retrieves none of the reference actions, while semantic retrieval preserves 96.0% and 97.0% candidate coverage, and the frozen selector adopts 97.34% of unseen skill types. A separate held-out repeat yields a consistent 9.0-point success gain and 18.98% token reduction, though the abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available to validate broader significance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Recursive LLM agents solve long-horizon tasks by decomposing them into subtasks, executing actions, and using environmental feedback. Leaf-level action grounding refers to translating an atomic subtask instruction into a concrete executable action, a step that often recurs across different tasks even when high-level plans differ. LeafSkill externalizes this grounding experience as reusable skills outside the model, so a small selector can bind action templates to current objects and admissible actions instead of calling a large model each time.

## [Async-RBench: Benchmarking Asynchronous Task Execution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32429) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Async-RBench, a benchmark of 200 executable tasks across eight scenarios that evaluates how LLM agents handle asynchronously arriving subagent results. The benchmark uses a scenario controller to regulate result delivery and an Event Response Contract (ERC) to specify required changes, preservations, avoidances, and re-verifications, scored by a new Dynamic Replanning Score (DRS) that weights process and outcome equally. As LLM agents increasingly run subagents asynchronously, final task success alone cannot reveal whether incoming results were handled correctly, so this benchmark fills a gap in evaluating reliable asynchronous execution. It provides a standardized way to measure response-process quality separately from outcome, which could guide the development of more robust agent architectures. Evaluating nine agents, the benchmark found DRS scores ranging from 19.2 to 45.1, with the lowest scores on duplicate reports and subagent failures. Under the execution protocol, all nine agents had lower task success with asynchronous delivery than with batched delivery (26.0% vs. 29.7% on average), and prompting the main agent to rebuild its plan after every result improved DRS by only 2.6 to 3.5 points for three agents.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to plan and execute tasks, often by spawning subagents that run in parallel. Asynchronous execution means the main agent continues working while subagents run, and results may arrive at unpredictable times, potentially changing the task state. Traditional benchmarks focus on final task success, but they do not capture whether the agent correctly integrated intermediate results, which is crucial for real-world reliability.

<a id="item-77"></a>
## [Martingale GRPO Truncates Failure Feedback in VLA Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32431) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Martingale GRPO (MART-GRPO), which uses success-probability and policy-score martingales to determine when to stop penalizing actions in vision-language-action reinforcement learning. A vision-language model fine-tuned with LoRA localizes the last visible error without observed recovery in rollout videos, and failure feedback is truncated after that point while preserving successful-rollout loss terms and original loss normalization. This addresses a known credit assignment problem in GRPO for VLA models, where a single trajectory-level advantage penalizes all actions including potentially useful recovery attempts. By preserving the gradient expectation while reducing covariance, MART-GRPO could improve training efficiency and success rates in robotic manipulation tasks. The truncation is theoretically justified because the removed gradient contribution has zero conditional expectation given the retained information, preserving the expectation of the reference GRPO group-gradient estimator. Experiments with OpenVLA-OFT on RoboTwin 2.0 show improved success rates over full-trajectory GRPO across multiple tasks, though the approach relies on a frozen VLM for error localization and retrospective boundary analysis.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm that assigns a single advantage to all actions in a trajectory based on group-relative rewards, and it was notably used to train DeepSeek-R1. Vision-language-action (VLA) models combine visual perception, language understanding, and action generation for robotics. A martingale is a stochastic process where the conditional expectation of the next value equals the current value, providing a mathematical tool to identify optimal stopping points.

## [ALLMTS: LLM Executes Clinical Guidelines for Safer Medical Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32469) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present ALLMTS, an LLM-based medical triage system that, instead of asking an LLM to directly decide dispositions, makes it select and execute commercially maintained telehealth guidelines. On 566 expert-validated synthetic scenarios, ALLMTS achieves 0.95 accuracy versus 0.47 for a raw-LLM baseline, and on a 50-scenario open benchmark it reaches 0.98 accuracy, on par with a five-nurse expert panel's 0.944 correct disposition rate. This work shows that coupling LLM language understanding with executable, auditable clinical guidelines can dramatically reduce over-triage—from up to 0.50 in baselines to at most 0.02—addressing a key safety concern in healthcare AI. It offers a practical blueprint for deploying LLMs in safety-critical triage while keeping decisions traceable to a clinical standard. The disposition follows deterministically from the first positive answer to the guideline's triage assessment questions, using the same traversal rule a nurse follows. Evaluation used synthetic, expert-validated scenarios spanning 14 dispositions, and a curated 50-scenario subset was released as an open benchmark; the approach still depends on the quality and coverage of the underlying commercial guidelines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Telephone triage is a safety-critical task in which a nurse must decide, from a brief intake note, whether a caller needs an ambulance, an emergency department visit, an appointment, or self-care at home. Large language models queried directly are unreliable for this task: they tend to over-escalate routine cases and cannot justify recommendations against an auditable clinical standard. ALLMTS instead grounds every disposition in the same commercially maintained telehealth guidelines that nurses use, turning the LLM into a guideline executor rather than a free-form decision-maker.

## [CauProphet: LLM Agent Learns Causal Cognition Like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce CauProphet, an LLM agent that builds and continuously updates a structured 'Causal Cognition' memory and a per-task 'Causal Belief' to guide decision-making. In financial forecasting it improves mean rank correlation with next-day returns by 25.4% over a baseline agent and raises prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Most LLM agents rely on retrieving past records or summaries, which limits their ability to reason about cause and effect and to transfer lessons across tasks. CauProphet's persistent causal knowledge could improve long-horizon decision-making and cross-task transfer, an important step toward more general and adaptive agents. The agent maintains a persistent Causal Cognition store and constructs a task-specific Causal Belief that captures causal factors and the effects of possible actions, updating both as new observations arrive. Evaluations cover financial research, scientific tasks, and everyday tasks, though the abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available yet.

## [CASE: Confusion-Aware Skill Evolution for Frozen Multimodal Emotion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CASE introduces a sparse, self-evolving library of pair-specific EmotionSkills that externalizes affective expertise from frozen multimodal emotion models to correct localized confusion between semantically adjacent emotions. On the CA-MER benchmark, it raises Qwen3-VL-8B's average accuracy from 46.80% to 59.13% while keeping the backbone frozen. This offers a parameter-efficient alternative to costly global fine-tuning for multimodal emotion recognition, and the learned skills transfer across different frozen MLLM backbones, potentially lowering adaptation costs for affective computing applications. CASE dynamically retrieves the appropriate EmotionSkill at inference time to reinforce boundary-aware reasoning, and experiments on CA-MER, DFEW, and EMER show consistent gains across both general-purpose and emotion-specialized MLLMs. The approach is a niche arXiv preprint with no community discussion provided.

These models often confuse semantically adjacent emotions (e.g., frustration vs. anger) because their multimodal cues overlap heavily. Conventional fixes require global parameter adaptation, which is expensive and inflexible, motivating agent-skill frameworks that modularize reusable expertise outside the model weights.

## [Paper argues agent failures stem from model-harness interaction, not just models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32483) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.32483) proposes a five-measure framework—Valid Completion Rate, Model-Harness Fit, Cross-Model Fit, Interaction Residuals, and Token Load—to evaluate LLM agent execution harnesses through their interactions with underlying models. The authors evaluated four general-purpose harnesses (OpenCode, Codex CLI, Pi CLI, DeepAgents) with four core models across six benchmarks, yielding 52,168 trajectories from 111 evaluation settings. This work challenges the common practice of attributing agent benchmark scores solely to the underlying model, showing that harness rankings vary across models and benchmarks and that task performance and valid completion can favor different model-harness pairings. It has implications for how agent evaluations are reported, how models are trained for harness use, and how harnesses are designed for specific models and tasks. The framework distinguishes task correctness from valid completion, and Interaction Residuals aim to surface pairing-specific strengths and weaknesses hidden by component-level summaries. OpenCode ranks first in cross-model performance, while DeepSeek V4 Flash Preview maintains consistently high Mean VCR across harnesses on the shared four-benchmark panel; the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are systems that combine a large language model with an execution harness—software that manages context, tool calls, planning, and artifact handoff—to complete multi-step tasks. Benchmarks for agents typically report a single score, which conflates the model's capabilities with the harness's design. Recent work (e.g., arXiv:2610.00917) has begun treating model-harness fit as a practical unit for evaluation, and this paper extends that line by proposing concrete measures and a large-scale empirical study.

## [IGG Benchmark Tests Interactive GUI Grounding Under Visibility Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32536) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Interactive GUI Grounding (IGG), a benchmark of 601 tasks across 16 controllable GUI environments and seven visibility-constraint sub-types, where an agent must first reveal a hidden target through scrolling, moving, dragging, or waiting before clicking it. In the human study participants reached 94.6% accuracy, while GPT-5.5 reached 79.4% and the strongest open-source VLM only 31.6%. Existing GUI grounding benchmarks assume the target is already visible in a static screenshot, so IGG exposes an unevaluated capability gap that matters for anyone building computer-use agents in real interfaces where targets are off-screen, occluded, or appear only after a state change. The large human-model gap signals that current VLMs are far from reliable autonomous GUI operation. The benchmark uses a compact action space of scrolling, moving, dragging, waiting, and clicking, and two controlled interventions show the bottleneck is execution rather than recognition: telling Qwen3-VL which interaction to perform raises accuracy only from 30.8% to 33.9%, whereas performing the interaction for the model raises it to 81.9%. The authors further show that visibility recovery is learnable and can be strengthened consistently across model families.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: GUI grounding is the task of locating a specific target element, such as a button or menu item, in a graphical user interface so that an agent can act on it. Vision-language models (VLMs) combine image understanding with text reasoning and are increasingly used to drive computer-use agents, but most evaluations feed them static screenshots in which the target is already visible. IGG instead evaluates the reveal step, requiring the agent to interact with the interface to make the target visible before attempting a click.

## [Experience Reuse Across Protein Pockets in LLM-Agent Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.32620) investigates whether an experience base built from discarded docking probes can transfer across protein pockets in LLM-agent-driven molecular optimization. Using the full log of one run over CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, the authors find that a key combining edit-site contacts, action, and round sharply separates improving from worsening edits on unseen pockets. This work offers a concrete empirical finding on how LLM agents can reuse past docking experience across different protein targets, which could improve the sample efficiency of agentic structure-based drug design. It also cautions that transferred signals mainly describe the edit and molecule rather than the target, suggesting experience bases may be better for prioritizing proposals than prescribing designs. The analysis is based on a single run over CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, and the observed separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed molecular size, the action, and the round—none of which describe the target. Naming the target outright separates edits several times more sharply, but for unseen pockets transfer must rely on descriptions; among exact-match descriptions, specific ones rarely recur while coarse ones provide little separation.

**Background**: LLM agents can optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by iteratively editing the molecule and docking each edit to estimate binding. CrossDocked2020 is a large public dataset of protein-ligand docking poses used to benchmark structure-based machine learning methods.

## [LGMT: Open-Source Tool Tests LLM Logical Reasoning Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32627) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LGMT is an open-source tool that uses first-order logic metamorphic relations to detect when LLMs give inconsistent answers to logically equivalent queries, without requiring ground-truth labels. It ships with 20 built-in relations, supports custom relations verified by the Z3 theorem prover, and revealed inconsistency rates of 20.84% to 29.44% across six widely used LLMs on a benchmark of 3,091 metamorphic groups. Static benchmarks evaluate queries in isolation and miss self-contradictions that can undermine LLMs used as reasoning components in software systems. LGMT offers a practical, label-free way to audit logical reliability, which matters for AI/ML engineers and anyone deploying LLMs in high-stakes reasoning pipelines. LGMT generates logically equivalent follow-up cases from a premise-conclusion source test case and checks whether the model's entailment predictions agree across the pair. It runs from a CLI or a web studio and includes an offline mock mode that requires no API key, making it accessible for reproducible testing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Metamorphic testing is a technique that checks software by relating inputs and outputs through properties that should hold, avoiding the need to know expected results. First-order logic provides formal rules for constructing logically equivalent statements, and the Z3 theorem prover, an SMT solver from Microsoft Research, can verify that custom metamorphic relations preserve logical equivalence.

A new arXiv paper (2610.32665) proposes an auto-consistency framework that tests whether tokens LLMs highlight as decisive for their predictions actually causally influence those predictions under targeted interventions. The study evaluates sufficiency, comprehensiveness, and counterfactuality metrics on IMDB and Steam reviews using GPT-4o-mini, Gemma3, Granite8B, and DeepSeek models. This work addresses a critical gap in explainability research by moving beyond textual plausibility to assess whether LLM-generated explanations are functionally consistent with model behavior. It could impact how practitioners trust and deploy LLM explanations in high-stakes text classification tasks. Results show that GPT-4o-mini follows the expected progression across all metrics, Gemma3 and Granite8B maintain coherence under sufficiency but lose consistency under more demanding interventions, while DeepSeek variants display structural deviations. Smaller models show contradictions and larger ones exhibit over-sensitivity, indicating explanation reliability varies across LLM families and scales.

## [Learned Speed Prior Retimes Robot Demonstrations for Faster, Smoother Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32676) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes learning a speed prior from large-scale demonstration data that predicts a distribution over execution speed for a given task instruction, motion path, and images, and uses a chosen quantile to define a consistent clock for retiming each demonstration. The method replaces per-task acceleration fitting with a single reusable model and is evaluated on 120 RoboLab tasks and a real robot cup-stacking task. Robot demonstrations often inherit operator-specific timing, causing policies to learn inconsistent speeds; a reusable speed prior could simplify data processing and improve policy performance across many tasks without per-task tuning. This is relevant to imitation learning and robotics practitioners seeking more efficient and generalizable training pipelines. The speed prior is fitted on both robot teleoperation data and handheld demonstrations where a human arm moves the gripper, and is used to retime robot demonstrations to the handheld pace before fine-tuning a vision-language-action policy. For execution, the policy is paired with a real-time optimal-control layer that tracks predicted action chunks under robot constraints; on 120 RoboLab tasks, policies fine-tuned with learned clocks succeed more often and move with up to half the jerk, and on a real robot the policy stacks cups in 3.50s (1.72× faster than the default 6.02s) while succeeding in 29 of 30 trials.

**Background**: In imitation learning, robots learn tasks by mimicking human demonstrations, but the speed of those demonstrations is often set by the teleoperator rather than the task, leading to inconsistent timing across demonstrations. Retiming is the process of adjusting the timing of a motion path to achieve a desired speed profile, and traditional methods require fitting acceleration signals per task. A speed prior learned from large-scale data aims to provide a general model of how fast tasks should be executed, eliminating the need for task-specific fitting.

Researchers present Agentic Hazard Mapper, a multi-agent system that emulates professional cartographic workflows and iterative design trade-offs to automatically generate hazard maps adapted to different data, locations, and hazard contexts. The system adapts design decisions to different data, locations, and hazard contexts, and the paper discusses human oversight, adaptation across users and communication contexts, and opportunities to extend agentic support across broader stages of hazard-map production and risk communication. Effective cartographic design requires expertise in symbolization, layout, and iterative trade-offs, which is not always available to local governments and communities. Multi-agent systems are AI architectures in which multiple specialized agents collaborate to complete complex workflows, and this paper applies that approach to emulate professional map-making.

## [ActionRAG: Graph Diffusion Refines Evidence for LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32752) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ActionRAG introduces a graph memory architecture for LLM agents that combines cautious indexing with query-time graph diffusion to refine evidence retrieval. On MemoryAgentBench, it achieves 55.1 average accuracy across eight deterministic-scoring columns, improving over the strongest retrieval-based memory baseline by 5.4 points. Long-term memory is a key bottleneck for LLM agents operating over growing interaction histories, and indexing errors and rigid similarity-based retrieval are common failure points. ActionRAG's approach of refining retrieval scores using graph structure could improve accuracy in memory-intensive agent tasks while keeping query-time costs low. The indexing pipeline reconciles type labels and applies tiered entity resolution to avoid fragmenting or merging entities. Query-time retrieval uses dual-channel entity and triple retrieval to form a bounded local graph, then applies receiver-normalized diffusion with seed protection and hub-penalized flux; mean synthesis cost stays below $0.001 per query, and retrieval-side p99 components sum to 440.7 ms on consumer Apple Silicon.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) typically retrieves passages by semantic similarity, but LLM agents with long-term memory need to recover facts that are related through chains of entities and relations. Graph-based memory represents facts as nodes and edges, enabling multi-hop reasoning, but errors in entity resolution during indexing can corrupt the graph. Graph diffusion is a technique that propagates scores over graph edges to refine initial estimates using local structure.

## [Enterprise RAG needs a knowledge layer that organizes before it retrieves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32851) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv paper presents an enterprise case study where a seven-agent reverse-engineering pipeline processed 241 repositories across 18 services into thousands of artifacts, and introduces a knowledge layer that organizes artifacts before retrieval to avoid near-duplicate rules from different services being conflated. This work shows that flat vector retrieval fails for multi-agent RAG in enterprises because recurring entities across services produce near-identical rules that are semantically similar but operationally distinct, and it offers a structural disambiguation approach that could improve reliability for SDLC agents and other enterprise AI systems. The knowledge layer consists of a self-describing catalog, a sparse metadata registry, and a navigation hypergraph defined over artifacts rather than extracted entities, so queries resolve to artifact identifiers that constrain vector search and make disambiguation structural rather than statistical; it was built in under a week and supported root-cause analysis of 100+ production defects and attribution of 35 release regression failures.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) typically uses flat vector retrieval, which matches queries to documents by semantic similarity. In enterprises, the same terms (e.g., offer, eligibility, activation) can appear across multiple services with different operational meanings, so similarity alone cannot distinguish them. A content-level ontology could disambiguate, but building one is costly and brittle; this paper instead places the ontology at the artifact level using provenance metadata.

## [LEMS and KFAC-SVD improve SVD-based LLM compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32878) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Layer-wise Error Modeling Search (LEMS) and KFAC-SVD to address global rank allocation and decomposition quality limitations in SVD-based LLM compression. LEMS uses a layer-wise error surrogate with local/global importance and propagation bias via ILP, while KFAC-SVD uses token-wise statistics to mitigate rank collapse, achieving up to 4.8 p.p. zero-shot accuracy gains and 15% average perplexity improvement on Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 up to 70B parameters. This work directly improves the practicality of low-rank compression for deploying large LLMs under memory and compute constraints, with gains that generalize to 70B-scale models. It provides a more principled approach to rank allocation and decomposition, potentially enabling wider adoption of SVD-based compression in production settings. LEMS formulates global rank allocation as an ILP problem using a layer-wise error surrogate that combines local and global layer importance with a propagation bias, while KFAC-SVD replaces Fisher-based estimators with token-wise statistics to avoid rank deficiency. The methods are validated across Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 families, with code and project page available at lems-svd.github.io.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: SVD-based low-rank compression approximates weight matrices with truncated singular value decomposition to reduce LLM size and compute. Prior methods often allocate ranks globally using uncalibrated error proxies and rely on Fisher-based estimators that can suffer from rank collapse, limiting compression quality. LEMS and KFAC-SVD aim to fix these issues by better modeling error propagation and using token-wise statistics.

## [NgramRL: Rollout-Aware N-gram Speculative Decoding Speeds Up RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32881) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NgramRL introduces a per-prompt n-gram drafter built from bounded recent rollout history to accelerate autoregressive generation during RL post-training of large language models, requiring no auxiliary model or drafter training. It also adds an adaptive fan-out prefetcher with a causal budget allocator, achieving 3.60–7.77 committed tokens per target-model forward and up to 1.99x more than SuffixDecoding. RL post-training is increasingly bottlenecked by autoregressive rollout generation, which can exceed 70% of training time, so a model-free acceleration method that preserves the rollout distribution could significantly reduce training costs for reasoning-focused LLMs. The approach avoids GPU memory overhead and drafter misalignment issues that plague model-based speculative decoding as the policy evolves. NgramRL uses a bounded recent rollout history per prompt and target-model verification to preserve the rollout distribution, with an adaptive fan-out prefetcher predicting verification outcomes (j, b) to hide CPU cache lookup latency. On Spider, the prefetcher covers 96.6% of Qwen3-8B outcomes at a candidate budget of 16 and improves measured rollout throughput by 1.38x over synchronous NgramRL, approaching its 1.40x overlap ceiling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by using a smaller drafter model to propose multiple tokens that a larger target model then verifies in parallel, but model-based drafters consume GPU memory and can become stale as the policy changes during RL training. N-gram or prompt-lookup drafters are model-free alternatives that reuse text patterns, yet existing versions do not explicitly exploit the repetition of rollouts across training epochs. RL post-training, such as with reinforcement learning from human feedback or verifiable rewards, repeatedly samples rollouts from the same prompts, creating a self-updating draft source.

## [Simplex-Constrained Embeddings Close the Modality Gap in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32890) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes replacing the unit-sphere embedding domain with the probability simplex to close the modality gap in multimodal contrastive models like CLIP and CLAP. By attaching lightweight softmax adapters and using Total Variation similarity, the method reduces the centroid L2 gap by 97–99% across five multimodal retrieval benchmarks while matching or exceeding standard retrieval performance. The modality gap is a persistent and well-documented flaw in contrastive multimodal models, affecting cross-modal retrieval, zero-shot transfer, and embedding interoperability. A purely geometric fix that requires only lightweight adapters could be broadly applicable to existing CLIP-style models without retraining from scratch, making it practically valuable for multimodal ML practitioners. The simplex and the unit sphere share the same intrinsic dimension, but the simplex's nonnegative, unit-mass constraints act as a structural prior that calibrates both modalities into the same coordinate system. The approach is purely geometric and relies on softmax adapters plus Total Variation similarity rather than architectural changes to the underlying encoders.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal contrastive models such as CLIP and CLAP are trained to map images, audio, and text into a shared embedding space, typically on the unit sphere. Despite strong performance, their embeddings for different modalities tend to occupy separate regions of that space—the so-called modality gap—which can hurt cross-modal alignment. The probability simplex is the set of nonnegative vectors summing to one, commonly used to represent probability distributions, and Total Variation distance is a standard measure of difference between such distributions.

## [SemantEq: Step-Restricted Equivalence for Faithful Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32919) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SemantEq introduces a new equivalence relation for checking whether autoformalized Lean 4 statements faithfully translate their natural-language sources, requiring each statement to transform into the other only through a chain of allowed steps like logical rearrangement and library rewriting. Its model-free search, SemantEq-Eval, achieves 100% precision and 65.6% recall on the ProofNetVerif test split, and SemantEq-ProofAudit rejects all 104 non-equivalent pairs while raising recall on 407 equivalent pairs from 65.4% to 81.1%. As AI models autoformalize mathematics at scale and provers grow stronger, correctness increasingly hinges on statement faithfulness rather than proof validity, and SemantEq closes a loophole where full logical equivalence trivially passes for any two provable statements. This work is relevant to both the AI-for-mathematics and formal verification communities because it provides a principled evaluation criterion that separates proof generation from proof acceptance. SemantEq judges proofs by the proof term Lean checks rather than the tactic script, and an 8.9-fold increase in CPU time adds at most three pairs, showing the search is stable. A language model finds more proofs but under two of four prompts proves both directions of about two in five non-equivalent pairs, which is why the separate ProofAudit acceptance policy is needed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Autoformalization is the task of translating informal mathematics from natural language into formal statements in a proof assistant such as Lean 4, a dependently typed interactive theorem prover and programming language. A common way to check faithfulness is to test logical equivalence against a trusted reference formalization, but full provability makes P ↔ Q trivially true for any two provable statements via P ↔ True ↔ Q. Earlier methods restrict the prover and require each proof to name the other statement, while SemantEq instead restricts the allowed transformation steps.

## [Overall Scorer Accuracy Can Mislead in Interactive LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31678) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.31678) shows that validating an automatic scorer with a single overall accuracy or F1 score can hide prompt-specific errors that distort comparisons of LLM responses across different follow-up prompts. In an empirical study with six models, 180 invented-entity questions, and six prompt conditions, the authors found that an apparent 9.3-point increase from asserting certainty fell to 2.3 points under two separate LLM raters, with the primary-rater interval including zero. This finding has direct implications for how researchers design and report LLM evaluations, as validating only the scorer as a whole can lead to inflated, shrunk, or even reversed measured effects. It urges the community to audit the exact difference they plan to report, including scorer and rater uncertainty, to ensure benchmark validity. In a balanced audit of 288 responses, overall F1 was .916, yet depending on the prompt, the scorer flagged 2.6%–33.3% of responses that the primary LLM rater judged not to contain an unsupported claim, and these prompt-specific estimates were uncertain. A second audit of 300 model-question cases under each prompt showed that two other prompt comparisons kept their direction under both raters, while an attempted prompt-specific correction remained too uncertain for a firm conclusion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: In LLM evaluation, researchers often use automatic scorers (e.g., LLM-as-a-judge) to label model responses, and they typically validate the scorer with an overall accuracy or F1 score. F1 score is a metric that combines precision and recall, but high overall performance can mask errors that vary by prompt condition. This paper derives how such prompt-specific errors alter a prompt comparison and empirically examines the problem, highlighting the need to validate the exact difference being reported rather than just the scorer as a whole.

## [Activation steering gains don't transfer to condition reuse in tool-use models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31686) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper audits residual-stream activation interventions in Qwen3.5-27B on tool-use tasks where time or permissions determine eligibility. Two case-specific residual-writing variants raised target-selection correctness from 35/128 to 119/128 and from 33/128 to 114/128, but these gains vanished when familiar facts and identifiers were recombined within the same selection task and yielded little improvement in independent eligibility judgments. This result is a cautionary finding for the interpretability and activation-steering community: improved target-selection correctness does not imply that the model genuinely uses the intended condition in other computations. It suggests that evaluations of edited internal states should test how the state is used, not only whether the trained answer is produced. The gains persisted across output formats but did not transfer to independent judgments about whether a candidate is eligible or should be selected. In a prespecified secondary comparison, an early-layer source patch changed 50/64 judgments when that source was used, with only one adverse change among 64 cases where it was not, yet this selective effect did not ensure correct complete actions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Activation interventions, including activation steering and residual-stream editing, modify a language model's internal activations at inference time to steer its behavior without retraining. The residual stream is the cumulative hidden-state pathway that transformer layers read from and write to, making it a common target for such interventions. Tool-use models must decide whether a tool is eligible based on conditions such as time or permissions and then select it, so correct selection is only one part of a correct complete action.

## [LLMs Can Align to Human Distributions but Generalization Is Unstable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31687) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.31687) studies how to achieve distributional alignment of LLMs with human reference distributions and how aligned models generalize to unseen samples, topics, and tasks. Using two large-scale benchmarks, the authors find that LLMs can match human distributions and generalize to unseen questions within the same domain, but cross-topic generalization depends on topic relationships and alignment can cause surprising degradations such as loss of creativity. This work matters because LLMs are increasingly used to simulate human behavior in surveys and social science research, where matching population distributions is essential for valid inference. The finding that alignment can unpredictably degrade other capabilities warns practitioners that distributional alignment is not a free lunch and requires careful validation beyond the target domain. The study evaluates alignment and generalization on two large-scale benchmarks, showing that within-domain generalization to unseen questions is reliable while cross-topic transfer hinges on semantic relatedness between topics (e.g., politics and institutional trust). Notably, aligned models can lose creative ability, indicating that generalization properties are inherently unpredictable and unstable.

**Background**: Distributional alignment means tuning a model so that its output distribution matches a target reference distribution, such as the distribution of answers in a human survey, rather than merely producing correct individual responses. Large language models are neural networks trained on vast text corpora to generate and analyze language, and they are increasingly used as stand-ins for human respondents. A central challenge is generalizing from small training samples to new questions and topics without distorting other model capabilities.
