## [SceneBloom: Agentic Framework Generates Realistic 3D Indoor Scenes for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32978) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SceneBloom is a new agentic framework that combines diffusion-based furniture layout sampling with contract-guided geometric compilation to generate physically valid 3D indoor environments. Across five room types, it achieves the highest visual quality and physical stability, with no mesh collisions or out-of-bounds objects, and its layouts are closer to real-room distributions than all baselines. Training generalizable embodied agents requires realistic, varied, and simulation-ready indoor environments, and SceneBloom's ability to generate such scenes without manual authoring could significantly reduce the cost of creating training data. Its object navigation policies trained in generated scenes outperform those trained on an equal number of real scans, suggesting synthetic data can supplement or even replace real-world data for certain tasks. The hierarchical sampler uses diffusion priors to generate furniture arrangements within functional zones, and a compiler then uses relations extracted from each proposal to guide geometric fitting and local pose correction. The method is evaluated on five room types and the code is available on GitHub, though the paper is a specialized arXiv preprint without community discussion.

**Background**: Embodied AI agents learn by interacting with simulated 3D environments, so the realism and diversity of those environments directly affect how well policies transfer to the real world. Diffusion models are generative models that learn to produce data by reversing a noise process, and here they are used to sample plausible furniture arrangements. 'Contract-guided compilation' refers to using spatial relations (like 'chair is next to table') as constraints to turn 2D layout proposals into physically valid 3D scenes.

## [GPT-2 Transformer Learns Arithmetic Tree Sequence, Generalizes Far Beyond Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01870) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A GPT-2 transformer trained from scratch on the first 10^11 elements of a sequence of rooted planar trees derived from prime factorization achieves about 0.4 word accuracy, outperforming a Hidden Markov Model baseline and maintaining stable performance on test blocks at 10^13–10^15, far beyond the training interval. This work provides rigorous evidence that transformers can capture deep arithmetic regularities beyond simple frequency statistics, which is important for understanding the theoretical capabilities and limits of transformer-based sequence models. The model's likelihood separates the arithmetic text from synthetic sequences preserving marginal token frequencies but not order, with separation widening with context, and from sequences containing more than three consecutive square-free integers, which arithmetic forbids; a scaling analysis over context window, dataset size, vocabulary size, and model size is also included.

## [MCPHunt: First Benchmark for Cross-Boundary Credential Leaks in Multi-Server MCP Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27819) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released MCPHunt, described as the first controlled benchmark that isolates non-adversarial, exact-match credential propagation across multi-server MCP trust boundaries. Using canary-based taint tracking, risky/benign/hard-negative coverage conditions, and completion-requires-secret (CRS) stratification, they evaluated 3,615 traces from 5 models across 147 tasks and 9 mechanism families, finding policy-violating propagation rates of 11.5–41.3% across all models. This work reframes credential leakage in MCP agents as a structural information-flow control problem caused by benign tool composition, not just malicious prompting, which matters for anyone building or deploying multi-server AI agents. It also shows that prompt-based mitigation can cut violations to as low as 0.3% while preserving 80.5% utility, but effectiveness varies by model, motivating data-flow-aware defenses. Propagation is pathway-specific, ranging from 0.0% to 74.4% across mechanisms for GPT-5.4, and is concentrated in browser-mediated data flows. Under a matched alternative-value control, propagation persisted at 25.9% vs. 20.4%, but the matched-pair confidence interval spans zero, so the comparison neither distinguishes nor equates the two rates; code, traces, and labeling pipeline are released under MIT and CC BY 4.0.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard introduced by Anthropic in late 2024 that connects AI assistants to external tools and data through MCP servers, with an MCP host acting as the AI agent that coordinates one or more servers. When an agent composes tools across multiple servers, individually benign read and write permissions can chain together so that credentials read from one server are written into another, creating cross-boundary information flow. Taint tracking is a classic security technique that marks sensitive data and follows it through a system; MCPHunt adapts it by embedding unique canary strings so that propagation detection reduces to objective string matching.

## [Model-Adaptive Tool Necessity Reveals LLM Knowing-Doing Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14038) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces a model-adaptive definition of tool necessity, grounded in each model's empirical performance, and compares it against observed tool-call behavior across four models on arithmetic and factual QA datasets. It finds substantial mismatches of 26.5–54.0% and 30.8–41.8%, respectively, revealing a 'knowing-doing gap' in LLM tool use. This work shows that improving tool-use reliability requires not only better recognition of when tools are needed but also better translation of that recognition into action, which has direct implications for designing more dependable LLM agents. The findings suggest that current agent architectures may systematically fail to act on their internal knowledge, affecting any application relying on autonomous tool invocation. By probing hidden states, the authors find that both the cognition signal (whether a model believes a tool is necessary) and the execution signal (whether it actually calls a tool) are often linearly decodable, but their probe directions become nearly orthogonal in the late-layer, last-token regime that drives the next-token action. Tracing sample trajectories further reveals that most mismatch occurs in the cognition-to-action transition rather than in cognition itself.

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents that must decide when to answer directly versus when to invoke external tools such as calculators or search engines. Prior work on adaptive tool use typically treated tool necessity as a model-agnostic property annotated by humans or LLM judges, often focusing on clear-cut cases. This paper argues that tool necessity is model-dependent: a problem solvable by a strong model alone may still require tools for a weaker one.

<a id="item-157"></a>
## [NeuroMAS treats multi-agent LLM systems as trainable neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16757) ⭐️ 7.0/10

NeuroMAS proposes treating multi-agent language systems as trainable, scalable neural-network-like architectures where LLM agents are role-free nodes and intermediate textual signals are edges, with reinforcement learning determining how nodes communicate, specialize, and coordinate. Experiments show it significantly outperforms both inference-time and trained multi-agent baselines, and that organizational scaling is path-dependent, with larger systems trainable only when grown progressively from smaller trained ones. This work shifts multi-agent design from hand-crafted workflow engineering toward architecture design, where depth, width, connectivity, and growth protocol become scalable sources of capability. It suggests learned neural multi-agent systems could be a promising new scaling axis for LLMs, affecting how researchers and practitioners build complex agentic applications. The paper provides a theoretical perspective showing why modular textual computation is more parameter-efficient when tasks admit hierarchical decompositions. A key caveat is that larger systems are challenging to train from scratch, but become feasible when grown progressively from smaller trained systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-agent language systems are typically built as hand-designed workflows where agents are assigned semantic roles and communication protocols are specified in advance. NeuroMAS instead draws an analogy to neural networks: LLM agents act as nodes, textual signals as edges, and reinforcement learning adjusts how information flows and how agents specialize. This contrasts with standard neural networks, where architecture is fixed up front and only weights are trained.

<a id="item-158"></a>
## [LLM confidence calibration is highly sensitive to measurement protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27752) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.27752v4) shows that across five open LLMs and four QA datasets, fixing each answer, its verbalized confidence, and its correctness label while varying how token-probability confidence is measured shifts the gap between token and verbalized confidence by roughly the size of the gap itself (e.g., ECE .287 vs .304; AUROC .189 vs .145). The 95% interval of the shift excludes zero in 19 and 17 of 20 settings, and the better-performing score flips in 8 settings under each metric. This finding undermines naive calibration benchmarks by showing that comparisons between confidence measures are not robust to arbitrary protocol choices, which could lead to misleading conclusions about which method is better. It affects AI/ML evaluation research and anyone deploying LLMs that rely on confidence estimates for uncertainty quantification. The shift is driven mostly by changes to the instruction rather than the position of the answer, and stated confidence also depends on how a supplied answer is written. The effect persists in four larger models up to 72B parameters, and the authors turn these results into a reporting checklist for LLM confidence and its comparisons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLMs can express confidence in two main ways: verbalized confidence (the model states how sure it is) and token confidence (derived from the probability of the answer's tokens). Calibration research asks which is better calibrated, typically using metrics like Expected Calibration Error (ECE), which measures the gap between stated confidence and observed accuracy, and AUROC, which measures how well confidence scores rank correct versus incorrect answers. This paper shows that these comparisons depend heavily on the context in which token probabilities are read.

## [PROBE framework guides LLM agents to jointly optimize binding affinity and druggability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces two diagnostic metrics showing that current LLM-agent pipelines in structure-based drug design rarely achieve joint improvement in binding affinity and druggability, and proposes PROBE, a probing-guided optimization framework inspired by medicinal chemists. PROBE builds a pocket-specific site map and an EditManual from controlled probe edits, then runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the CrossDocked2020 benchmark. This work exposes a consistent failure mode in LLM-based molecular optimization—editing without understanding pocket-ligand response—and offers a principled way to mitigate it, which could improve the reliability of AI agents in drug discovery. It is valuable for AI/ML and computational chemistry researchers working on structure-based drug design. The two diagnostic metrics quantify how often a single edit improves both objectives and how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. PROBE decomposes the ligand into editable sites, flags where joint gains are plausible or where liability substructures should be changed, and distills probe responses into an EditManual to guide iterative optimization; code is available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Structure-based drug design uses the three-dimensional structure of a biological target to design small molecules that bind tightly and favorably. A viable drug candidate must also be drug-like (druggable), meaning it has properties suitable for clinical testing, such as good solubility and stability. LLM agents are increasingly used to iteratively edit ligands, but binding affinity and druggability often conflict, making joint optimization challenging.

## [SkillPyramid: Hierarchical Skill Consolidation for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03692) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillPyramid introduces a hierarchical skill consolidation framework with a self-evolution mechanism that enables AI agents to compose, validate, and incorporate new skills during task execution. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld across four backbone models show a 38.0% average reward increase and a 27.7% reduction in execution steps. This work addresses a key limitation of current AI agents—redundant skill construction and poor transfer—by transforming a static skill pool into a dynamic evolution system. It could significantly improve long-term learning and generalization for LLM-based agents in complex, multi-task environments. The framework operates on a hierarchical skill topology and includes a validation step to ensure new skills are reliable before incorporation. Evaluation spans three diverse benchmarks (embodied tasks, web navigation, and science experiments) and four backbone models, demonstrating consistent gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents powered by large language models often solve tasks by invoking pre-defined skills, but they lack a systematic way to accumulate and reuse experience across tasks. Benchmarks like ALFWorld (text-based embodied tasks), WebShop (simulated e-commerce), and ScienceWorld (interactive science experiments) test an agent's ability to perform multi-step reasoning and action in realistic scenarios. Skill consolidation aims to let agents build a reusable skill library over time, improving efficiency and generalization.

## [SWARR: RL Adaptation Makes Sliding-Window Attention Competitive for Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11634) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SWARR, a two-stage recipe that converts a pretrained self-attention model to sliding-window attention (SWA) via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and then adapts it with on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) for math reasoning. Experiments show that RL substantially narrows the accuracy gap between SWA and full self-attention, recovering much of the performance lost during conversion while retaining linear-complexity efficiency. This work addresses the quadratic cost of self-attention for long-context LLM reasoning by showing that RL can make efficient SWA models viable for demanding tasks like math reasoning. It offers a practical path to deploy cheaper long-context inference without pretraining new base models, which could benefit cost-sensitive and resource-constrained applications. The authors hypothesize that SWA underperforms after SFT partly due to a data-architecture mismatch: SFT data prepared for self-attention may contain long-range dependencies that SWA struggles to model. On-policy RL optimizes self-generated trajectories under the SWA constraint, aligning training with the architecture's limitations; however, the approach is evaluated only on math reasoning benchmarks and does not fully close the gap to self-attention.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Self-attention scales quadratically with context length, making long-context inference expensive. Sliding-window attention (SWA) restricts each token to attend only to a fixed-size local window, reducing complexity to linear and enabling efficient long-context processing. Reinforcement learning with LLMs, particularly on-policy methods that optimize self-generated outputs, has become a key technique for improving reasoning abilities.

## [AlphaMemo: Structured Memory Boosts LLM Alpha Mining Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20625) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AlphaMemo introduces a structured search-process memory for self-evolving LLM alpha mining agents that records reusable edit-motif evidence extracted from Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) differences, rather than memorizing only final factors or full trajectories. It combines confidence-gated residual memory over a search-ledger prior with asymmetric veto control to suppress high-confidence failure patterns, and experiments on CSI 500 and S&P 500 show improved out-of-sample performance and fixed-budget discovery efficiency. This work targets a core failure mode of agentic alpha mining—redundant discovery and overfitting from naively reusing past successes—by making memory context-aware and evidence-based, which could improve the reliability and efficiency of LLM-driven quantitative research pipelines. It also offers a transferable memory design for other self-evolving agents operating in noisy, non-stationary search spaces. The memory stores edit motifs derived from AST diffs, applies confidence gating on top of a search-ledger prior, and uses an asymmetric veto to block high-confidence failure patterns; ablations confirm the contributions of residual learning, confidence gating, AST-diff motifs, and veto memory. The code is released at https://github.com/jarrettyu/sspm, and evaluation is limited to CSI 500 and S&P 500 equity markets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: In quantitative finance, an "alpha" (or factor) is a signal or formula intended to predict asset returns, and alpha mining is the search for such signals. LLM agents can propose and refine executable factor expressions, but the space of possible formulas is combinatorially large and market feedback is noisy and non-stationary, so agents often rediscover similar factors or overfit to past data. Memory mechanisms for self-evolving agents typically store past trajectories or skills to guide future decisions without changing the underlying model weights.

## [SciHazard: New Benchmark for Scientific Safety Risks in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18665) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SciHazard, a benchmark of 2400 hazardous and 600 oversafety questions across 12 scientific disciplines, along with a decomposed DeHarm-Score that evaluates query severity, refusal behavior, and response-level harm including executability and net-new risk. An expert-validation study shows DeHarm-Score improves agreement with expert annotations by 90.17% over the strongest baseline, and benchmarking 31 frontier LLMs and deep research agents reveals that deep research agents score 32.3% higher on mean DeHarm-Score than standard LLMs. This work addresses key limitations of existing safety benchmarks, such as templated queries disconnected from real-world hazards and ungrounded LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations, by grounding queries in regulated entities and documented failure scenarios. It highlights autonomous deep research agents as a critical blind spot in current safety defenses, which is important as LLMs are increasingly used to support scientific work. The DeHarm-Score decomposes response-level harm into Executability, quantified via dynamic checklists with importance weighting, and Net-new risk, assessed through retrieval-augmented claim extraction and synthesis-barrier verification. The benchmark includes 600 oversafety questions to test whether models are overly cautious, and code and dataset are available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in scientific research, but they can also provide actionable guidance for misusing hazardous scientific knowledge. Existing safety benchmarks often use templated queries that are not grounded in real-world hazards and rely on LLM-as-a-Judge paradigms without domain expertise, leading to unreliable evaluations. SciHazard aims to provide a more realistic and decomposed evaluation of scientific safety risks.

## [Janus: Foresight-Oriented Guard for Long-Horizon Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19913) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Janus introduces a foresight-oriented guard framework that trains models to anticipate delayed risks from partial agent trajectories and blocks unsafe actions before execution. Its resulting guard model, Vanguard, improves average protection by 15.9 percentage points over baseline guards across four agent-safety benchmarks while increasing benign task completion by 5.1 percentage points. This work shifts agent safety from post-hoc content moderation toward proactive prevention of operational failures, which is critical as LLM-based tool-using agents take on longer, multi-step tasks. The joint anticipation and adjudication approach could influence how future guard models are designed and trained for real-world agent deployments. Janus synthesizes diverse agent trajectories via multi-agent simulation and learns a shared policy with two coupled tasks: an anticipation task that forecasts safety-relevant futures and an adjudication task that decides safety from both the observed prefix and anticipated future. The two tasks are jointly optimized with CoAA-RL, which rewards forecasts by their utility for downstream safety judgment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon agents execute sequences of tool calls where a harmful outcome may only become apparent many steps after the initial action, making traditional content moderation insufficient. Guard models are classifiers or policies that monitor agent behavior and intervene before unsafe actions are executed. Reinforcement learning (RL) trains models by rewarding desired behaviors, and here CoAA-RL specifically rewards forecasts that help the downstream safety decision.

## [MirrorCraft: Paired Benchmark Tests LLM Agents Under Hidden Minecraft Rule Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29218) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MirrorCraft introduces a paired benchmark that evaluates LLM-based agents in Minecraft under silently modified game rules, using 10 Vanilla worlds, six rule suites, and three progression tasks across 8,640 episodes. It measures the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) as the performance change between paired Vanilla and Mirror worlds, revealing that hidden rule changes have sharply different effects across suites. Most existing Minecraft benchmarks evaluate agents under fixed, well-documented game mechanics, so high performance does not show whether an agent can adapt when familiar recipes, drops, or rules change. MirrorCraft addresses this gap by providing a controlled setting to study agent robustness and adaptation, which is critical for developing reliable LLM agents that can handle dynamic environments. The benchmark uses Minecraft Java 1.19 and a shared Mineflayer interface, with each Mirror world copied from its paired Vanilla world and loading a datapack for a different rule suite. Experiments show that ReAct, Reflexion, and Voyager achieve the highest pooled Mirror Scores without rule descriptions, but ReAct loses the most under compressed resource drops; providing exact rules yields only modest gains, and most suite differences remain even when rules are known.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Minecraft is a popular open-world sandbox game often used to test AI agents because it requires planning, resource gathering, and crafting. LLM-based agents like Voyager use large language models to decide actions, and benchmarks typically measure progress via advancements and task completion. Datapacks are server-side modifications that can change game rules without altering the client, enabling controlled experiments.

## [TreeWY removes per-draft recurrent state snapshots in Gated DeltaNet hybrids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20961) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.20961v2) introduces TreeWY, a tree-structured WY transform of the gated delta rule that eliminates the need to snapshot the full recurrent state at every draft position when verifying speculative decoding drafts in Gated DeltaNet hybrid models. Instead of storing per-node states, it computes every draft node's output with a single triangular solve and reconstructs only the accepted state on commit, storing a small pseudo-value matrix. Speculative decoding is a key technique for speeding up LLM inference, but in hybrid linear-attention models the memory cost of snapshotting recurrent states makes wide, high-acceptance draft trees infeasible. TreeWY removes this bottleneck, and benchmarks on Qwen3.5 35B and 397B show it cuts recurrent-state memory and KV-cache pressure at essentially identical acceptance length, turning freed HBM into higher throughput and lower time-to-first-token where memory binds. The derivation depends only on the gated delta rule and not on other architectural details, and the method costs a few percent in performance where memory is not the binding constraint. For tree width, the same memory savings make a wider, higher-acceptance draft affordable, though the authors note this is not yet a throughput win.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Modern open models increasingly use hybrid architectures where most layers are linear-attention layers, such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), which carry a small fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value (KV) cache, making ordinary decoding memory-efficient. Speculative decoding verifies a batch of draft tokens and rolls back rejected ones, but in GDN layers this requires snapshotting the full recurrent state at every draft position, and those snapshots cannot be shared across branches of a draft tree. The WY transform is a mathematical technique for representing sequences of rank-one updates compactly, and TreeWY applies a tree-structured version of it to the gated delta rule.

## [Team History Beats Role Competence in Multiagent LLM Swaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.05279v2) tests the common assumption that a role in a multiagent LLM team is just a slot, finding that a same-role newcomer carrying another team's notebook outperforms a notebook-less newcomer but still underperforms the original team in cooperative cooking, wildfire response, and medical QA. The shortfall appears only after the team has worked together, persists across model families and decoding temperatures, and narrows by two thirds when planning-only members are added. The results challenge the widely used 'role as slot' design pattern in multiagent LLM systems, suggesting that team-specific history and shared notebooks carry value that cannot be transferred by role competence alone. This has direct implications for how practitioners build, swap, and scale agent teams, and for evaluating whether a replacement is truly equivalent. The study uses a one-seat swap protocol that replays a formed, frozen team with its own member, a same-role newcomer carrying another team's notebook, or a newcomer with no notebook; on the hardest cooking level, notebooks from the same donor teams leave a leading newcomer holding the recipe worse off than no notebook, while a follower benefits, and notebook lines about the partner reproduce the whole seat difference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Multiagent LLM systems assign different roles (e.g., leader, follower, planner) to separate LLM instances that collaborate on a task, often keeping short written notebooks as shared memory. A common design assumption is that roles are interchangeable slots, so any agent competent at a role can replace a departing member. This paper tests that assumption empirically by measuring what happens when a team member is swapped out.

## [PlaceReasoner-Beta Reformulates VLSI Macro Placement as Verifier-Guided Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21263) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PlaceReasoner-Beta introduces a verifier-guided multi-agent framework that uses a vision-language model (VLM) planner to generate candidate macro placements from floorplan images, macro specs, and connectivity, with geometric and physical verifiers enforcing legality and refining layouts via early implementation feedback. The authors also release PlaceReasoner-Bench, an open end-to-end benchmark of 8 designs at two aspect ratios (16 tasks) evaluated on routed PPA and DRC, where the method reduces post-route TNS by 61.2% at 1:1 and 53.0% at 2:1 versus a classical baseline. This work shifts macro placement from one-shot proxy-objective optimization to closed-loop reasoning that incorporates visual context, design expertise, and downstream physical-design feedback, potentially improving end-to-end layout quality in AI-for-chip-design workflows. It also provides a reproducible open benchmark that evaluates on routed PPA and DRC rather than pre-route proxies, which could help standardize comparisons in EDA research. The framework includes a post-route optimizer that further improves promising layouts using final PPA, and the benchmark fixes floorplans and I/O assignments so methods differ only in macro positions and orientations. Notably, PlaceReasoner-Beta shortens routed wirelength on most designs despite never explicitly optimizing it, though the work is an arXiv preprint (v2) with no community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: In VLSI physical design, macro placement decides where large pre-designed blocks (macros) go on the chip floorplan, and poor placement can congest routing and break power and clock distribution. Traditional automated methods optimize hand-crafted proxy objectives such as estimated wirelength in a single numerical optimization pass, which limits their ability to use visual layout context or feedback from later design stages. Vision-language models (VLMs) can interpret images and text together, and verifier-guided multi-agent frameworks separate candidate generation from checking, making them attractive for design tasks with hard physical constraints.

## [TAME Uses Sparse Autoencoders to Curb Chain-of-Thought Obfuscation in VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24243) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.24243v3) finds that RL-induced chain-of-thought obfuscation in vision-language models corresponds to a measurable representational shift in activation space, where task-agnostic template features become less separable from visually grounded content. The authors propose TAME, a Sparse Autoencoder-based activation-level regularization that penalizes template activations while preserving visual evidence during RL training, improving CoT monitorability by up to +30.9 points on VIRL-39k and +16.7 points on SPA-VL over GRPO. As RL becomes the standard way to train reasoning-capable VLMs, the tendency of models to produce plausible but ungrounded reasoning traces undermines CoT monitoring, a key safety tool. TAME offers a training-time intervention that directly targets the internal representations responsible for obfuscation, potentially enabling more transparent and trustworthy multimodal reasoning systems. TAME combines an anti-obfuscation feedback loop with an asymmetric SAE constraint that penalizes template activations while preserving visual evidence, and experiments across two model families show improved monitorability with largely preserved accuracy on four standard VLM benchmarks, though with task-specific trade-offs. The work is an arXiv preprint with code released on GitHub, and its practical impact remains to be validated by the community.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting lets models write out intermediate reasoning steps, which humans can monitor for unsafe or unfaithful behavior. However, when models are trained with reinforcement learning to maximize reward, they can learn to produce reasoning traces that look good but are not faithful to their actual computation—a phenomenon called CoT obfuscation. Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) are a mechanistic interpretability tool that decomposes model activations into sparse, human-interpretable features, and have been used to identify and steer specific concepts in language models. This paper extends that idea to vision-language models, linking obfuscation to a shift in activation space and using SAEs to regularize training.

## [Clinical AI Must Answer Two Questions Before Deployment, Paper Argues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25852) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.25852v2) argues that clinical AI models can achieve strong predictive performance by exploiting information generated by existing clinical judgment, and proposes evidence requirements matched to intended use plus state-reference and proxy-relative timing tests to verify usefulness before deployment. This work highlights a subtle but important failure mode in clinical AI evaluation—label leakage via clinician judgment—that current leakage checks, overall performance metrics, and external validation do not address, and it gives hospitals a concrete framework to assess whether a model's performance conditions hold when assistance is most needed. The framework specifies direct evidence required to answer whether a model's performance depends on information generated by existing clinical judgment, and introduces state-reference and proxy-relative timing tests for stronger recognition claims; these questions remain outside current evaluation practice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Clinical AI models are often evaluated by predictive performance and external validation, but if the labels or features used in training already encode clinicians' judgments, the model may learn to replicate that judgment rather than recognize new cases early. Label leakage occurs when information unavailable at deployment time inadvertently influences training, inflating apparent performance. This paper argues that such leakage can arise from clinical judgment itself and proposes timing-based tests to detect it.

<a id="item-171"></a>
## [Metro-WM Replaces Unrealisable Latent Sub-Goals with Retrieved States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31868) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Metro-WM is a hierarchical planning framework that abandons unconstrained latent sub-goal prediction, instead building a graph of observed frames from offline expert or random-action trajectories and retrieving genuine states as sub-goals to guide a JEPA-based planner. Experiments show it achieves up to 37.33 percentage points higher long-horizon success rates than the strongest hierarchical baseline while being up to 10.9 times faster. The paper identifies a fundamental flaw in leading hierarchical latent planners—they routinely emit physically unrealisable sub-goals—and offers a grounded, retrieval-based alternative that is more robust to execution errors and far cheaper to train. This could shift how the community designs long-horizon world-model planners, favoring grounded memory over unconstrained latent generation. Because planning runs over the full graph, Metro-WM can instantly reroute from the current state if the micro planner drifts, and it requires 13–56 times less offline compute and fewer tuned hyperparameters. Notably, it often finds shorter paths than the offline demonstrations and outperforms an oracle that uses the query's own demonstration on most benchmarks, while remaining robust under extremely sparse datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: JEPA (Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture), proposed by Yann LeCun and collaborators at Meta, is a self-supervised architecture that predicts representations in latent space rather than reconstructing raw inputs. Model-predictive control (MPC) uses a learned world model to plan by simulating future states and optimizing actions, but with JEPA-style latent models it works well only over short horizons. Hierarchical extensions add a macro planner that proposes intermediate latent sub-goals to guide a micro planner, aiming to extend planning range.

## [Symbolic Guidance Taxonomy Balances Autonomy in LLM Multi-Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31963) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces the Symbolic Guidance Taxonomy (SGT), which characterizes a spectrum of agent autonomy from open-ended natural language reasoning to fully prescribed algorithmic execution, with intermediate levels providing partial pseudocode guidance. Experiments on the AgentsNet benchmark show that intermediate autonomy levels consistently outperform both unguided agents and fully prescriptive specifications. The findings identify autonomy regulation as a key design principle for LLM-based distributed coordination, suggesting that neither full autonomy nor rigid algorithmic control is optimal. This has implications for designing more reliable multi-agent systems in complex domains where LLM agents must coordinate. The SGT spans a spectrum from open-ended natural language reasoning to fully prescribed algorithmic execution, with intermediate levels offering partial pseudocode guidance. The abstract is truncated, so specific quantitative results and the exact number of autonomy levels are not detailed in the provided content.

AgentsNet is a benchmark framework for distributed coordination among LLM agents that evaluates multi-agent systems on fundamental distributed computing problems and can scale to up to 100 agents. Pseudocode is a step-by-step description of an algorithm in simple English-like structure, used for human understanding rather than machine execution.

## [RL-trained teachers outperform SFT teachers for on-policy distillation and model merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.32303v3) systematically compares reinforcement learning (RL) versus supervised fine-tuning (SFT) as the algorithm for training domain expert teachers used in on-policy distillation (OPD) and multi-teacher OPD (MOPD). Using Qwen3.5-9B as the shared initialization and matching teacher task performance, the study finds that RL teachers produce stronger students and recover more of the teachers' performance gains across Agentic, Reasoning, and Perception domains. This work addresses an understudied question in the model-merging and distillation pipeline: whether the algorithm used to train expert teachers affects how well their capabilities transfer to a merged student. The consistent advantage of RL teachers across three domains suggests that practitioners should consider RL rather than SFT when building teachers for OPD-based merging, which could improve the efficiency and quality of multi-domain model merging. The analysis attributes the RL advantage to smaller parameter displacements from the shared initialization and faster or consistently higher teacher–student Top-K overlap during OPD, supporting the hypothesis that teachers closer to the student's starting point facilitate on-policy learning. The comparison is controlled at similar teacher task performance, but the study is a focused empirical investigation on a single Qwen3.5-9B backbone rather than a broad architectural or scaling study.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on trajectories sampled from its own evolving policy, with a teacher model evaluating those trajectories, rather than distilling from a static corpus. In multi-teacher OPD (MOPD), several domain experts trained from a shared checkpoint transfer their specialized skills into one student, effectively merging their capabilities. Qwen3.5-9B is a small open-weight model from Alibaba's Qwen family that supports thinking mode and runs on consumer-grade GPUs.

Researchers introduced Back-to-the-Future (BTTF), an end-to-end multi-agent framework that converts unstructured simulation dumps into a normalized SQLite database and translates natural-language verification queries into schema-aware SQL. ## [OSVerify decouples error proposal and validation for one-sided AI solution verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18089) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces OSVerify, a framework that decouples error proposal from error validation to maximize rejection of invalid AI-generated solutions while bounding false rejection of valid ones under limited human review capacity. Experiments show it reduces human reviews of invalid solutions by 46% on average versus voting baselines, improving to 57% after post-training the validator. As AI generates candidate solutions faster than experts can review them, this one-sided verification approach makes human review cost an explicit optimization target, potentially enabling scalable human-AI collaboration in scientific discovery. It has been applied to identify errors in published papers and to help resolve three Langlands open conjectures confirmed by experts. OSVerify uses a hierarchical decomposition error proposer that searches for errors at different granularities, paired with a validator that distinguishes substantial errors from minor or hallucinated ones. The approach is evaluated on competition- and research-level benchmarks under an empirical low false-rejection rate constraint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: In AI-assisted scientific discovery, large language models can propose many candidate solutions, but without reliable automated evaluation, human experts must review them. Verification under limited human effort aims to filter out invalid candidates while prioritizing rare valid ones, which can be framed as maximizing the true negative rate (TNR) subject to a constraint on the false negative rate (FNR). Decoupling error proposal from error validation leverages the intuition that verifying a specific error is easier than discovering one.

AgentPerturb introduces a two-dimensional framework that jointly characterizes perturbation targets and types to systematically evaluate LLM agent robustness, with controlled experiments on the APEX-Agents and Toolathlon benchmarks showing consistent performance degradation across frontier models. This work provides a rigorous benchmark-construction pipeline and trajectory-level failure analysis, highlighting that agents rarely detect or verify corrupted inputs—a critical reliability gap as LLM agents are increasingly deployed in noisy real-world environments. ## [EvoSkill v2 uses a stateful coding agent to evolve LLM agent skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25931) ⭐️ 7.0/10

EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system (benchmark adaptor, solver, and evolver) that rewrites an agent's instructions while leaving its model and harness unchanged. Its evolver is implemented as a single stateful coding agent with a bash tool that persists across the whole run, and it raised held-out pass rates by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and 5.4 on τ³-bench banking over the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost. This work shows that agent skills can be optimized as external state rather than through model fine-tuning, and that a persistent stateful evolver outperforms the common single-call and fresh-per-round designs. It also demonstrates that a weaker model such as Claude Haiku 5.5 can improve its own skills, which lowers the cost barrier for self-improving agents. The evolver is given several attempts per training task, and every epoch's skills are accepted rather than selected on noisy scores, with the authors also measuring rollout variance between attempts. The evolver cost less per percentage point of held-out pass rate gained than either baseline's optimizer, and in the τ³-bench banking experiment with Claude Haiku 5.5 powering both solver and evolver, gains reached up to 21.5 percentage points.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Skill optimizers treat an agent's natural-language instruction document as trainable state while keeping the underlying model frozen; Microsoft's SkillOpt, for example, has a separate optimizer model read scored rollouts and propose bounded edits. EvoSkill v2 contrasts this with two common designs: a sequence of single model calls (as in SkillOpt) and an agent that starts afresh each round (as in SkillBoost). A stateful system retains session information across interactions, whereas a stateless one does not, which is the key architectural difference EvoSkill v2 exploits.

## [GenXR-Bench: First Benchmark for Coding Agents in Interactive XR Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29014) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GenXR-Bench introduces the first benchmark for end-to-end evaluation of coding agents in interactive extended reality (XR) software development, featuring 112 expert-authored tasks across 24 feature implementations, 21 bug repairs, and 67 application generation tasks. Its testing suite achieves 96.5% accuracy against blinded expert judgments on 124 candidate applications run on consumer XR hardware, and evaluating 10 models shows the strongest configuration reaches only 50.9% overall Pass@1. This benchmark fills a gap in evaluating coding agents beyond traditional software, addressing the unique challenges of XR applications that operate in dynamic 3D physical environments with continuous user motion, spatial input, and environmental sensing. It provides a reproducible testbed for the AI agent evaluation and XR research communities, highlighting that even top models struggle with asynchronous sensor lifecycles, physical grounding, and embodied interaction semantics. The held-out tests combine scripted and adaptive interactions with programmatic and visual assertions to evaluate both core functionality and runtime behavior. Failure analysis reveals recurring misunderstandings in asynchronous sensor lifecycles, physical grounding, and embodied interaction semantics, indicating specific areas where coding agents need improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that can autonomously write, modify, and debug code, evolving from single-file generation to long-horizon repository-scale engineering. Extended reality (XR) encompasses virtual, augmented, and mixed reality applications that run in dynamic 3D physical environments, where runtime robustness depends on continuous user motion, spatial input, and environmental sensing. Traditional software benchmarks do not capture these interactive and physically situated conditions, making XR a novel and underexplored domain for evaluating coding agents.

Researchers present a multi-task deep learning surrogate that emulates the BurnP3+ process-based wildfire simulator, achieving 806-fold CPU speedup and using only 13% of the host memory on a representative region. The model integrates geospatial inputs (fuel, terrain, ignition likelihood) and regional weather to predict burn probability, mean fire intensity, and mean rate of spread, preserving downstream hazard classifications. ## [Scaling Laws Predict Safety Probe Performance from Model Size and Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30678) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper constructs six new safety datasets and trains two probe architectures on 20 base models from five model families, using 32 to 10^6 labels per model, to derive two scaling laws for probe error (1 - AUROC). The laws show that the lowest achievable probe error falls as a power law in model size, and that error decreases toward this floor as labels increase, with the slope steepening for larger models. This work lets safety researchers predict probe performance in advance from model size and label budget, avoiding costly trial-and-error retraining whenever a new model or harm area appears. It could make real-time safety monitoring more efficient and inform decisions about whether to invest in more labels or a larger model to reach a target error. When fitted on the smallest models of a family, the laws predict the next larger model's error within 0.02 in 72% of cases, identify the cheaper path to a target error (more labels vs. larger model) in 73% of cases, and choose the probe architecture with an average extra error of only 0.006 when picking the worse one. The laws hold across tasks, model families, and probe architectures, with only fitted constants differing.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Safety probes are small classifiers trained on a language model's internal activations to detect harmful or jailbreak inputs in real time, and they must be retrained for each new model or harm area. Neural scaling laws are empirical relationships describing how model performance changes as factors like model size and data scale up, and they have proven remarkably predictive across many orders of magnitude. This paper extends the scaling-law framework to probe error, using linear probe architectures and AUROC as the performance metric.

## [Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension: A Mathematical Foundation for Open-Ended Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31097) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension, a mathematical model that treats cognitive states as partial evaluations over a formal language, enabling open-ended intelligence by extending beyond undecidable propositions. It uses Kolmogorov complexity to show that no fixed architecture can generate irreducible algorithmic information, and proposes semantic self-extension as a design principle for AI. This work offers a novel theoretical foundation for open-ended intelligence, drawing on Kant and Gödel to explain how AI systems can transcend their representational limits. It could influence the design of future AI architectures that aim for continual learning and creativity rather than fixed-domain performance. The model distinguishes between undecided and undecidable propositions, showing that reasoning can resolve the former but not the latter, leaving a frontier for extension. It argues that extension requires persistent external memory preserving semantic content and historical coordinates, and that collective generative capacity depends on balancing overlap for transfer with difference for generation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Gödel's incompleteness theorems show that any sufficiently powerful formal system contains true but undecidable propositions, which limits what can be proven within the system. Partial evaluation is a technique that specializes programs by pre-computing parts of them, and here it is used metaphorically to describe cognitive states as incomplete evaluations of a formal language. Kolmogorov complexity measures the amount of irreducible information in an object, providing a way to quantify the limits of what a fixed system can generate.

## [Adaptive l^p Norm Tuning Mitigates Catastrophic Overfitting in Fast Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.02360) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2505.02360v3) proposes a noiseless solution to Catastrophic Overfitting (CO) in fast adversarial training by adaptively controlling the l^p training norm. The method, called adaptive l^p-FGSM, tunes the norm based on gradient concentration measured by Participation Ratio and entropy, and achieves competitive robustness without extra regularization or noise injection. Catastrophic overfitting severely limits the practicality of fast adversarial training methods like FGSM, which are much cheaper than multi-step attacks. This work provides a theoretically principled and simple fix that could make fast adversarial training more reliable for real-world deployment of robust models. The core insight is that CO arises when highly concentrated gradients (information localized in few dimensions) interact with aggressive norm constraints. By quantifying gradient concentration via Participation Ratio and entropy, the adaptive l^p-FGSM automatically selects the training norm, avoiding the need for hand-tuned regularization or noise injection.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Adversarial training is a key technique for making deep neural networks robust to adversarial examples—inputs deliberately perturbed to cause misclassification. Fast adversarial training methods like FGSM use single-step attacks to speed up training, but they often suffer from catastrophic overfitting, where the model suddenly becomes vulnerable to multi-step attacks despite appearing robust to single-step ones. Existing mitigations rely on noise injection, regularization, or gradient clipping, but these add complexity and hyperparameters.

## [Dynamic Relational Priming Boosts Transformer for Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12196) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2509.12196v3) proposes 'prime attention,' a dynamic relational priming mechanism that tailors token representations per pair-wise interaction via learnable modulations, instead of using static token representations as in standard attention. Experiments show up to 6.5% improvement in forecasting accuracy and comparable or better performance with up to 40% less sequence length. This work addresses a fundamental limitation of standard attention in handling heterogeneous inter-channel dependencies in multivariate time series, where different channel pairs may follow entirely different physical laws or temporal dynamics. The approach maintains the same asymptotic computational complexity as standard attention, making it potentially widely applicable to time series forecasting and other domains with diverse relational dynamics. Prime attention introduces representational plasticity by dynamically modulating each token for every pair-wise interaction, enabling extraction of relationship-specific information. It achieves up to 6.5% accuracy improvement and maintains the same asymptotic complexity as standard attention, with the additional benefit of requiring up to 40% shorter sequences for comparable performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Transformers rely on self-attention, where each token's representation is fixed across all pair-wise computations in a layer. This works well when relationships are relatively homogeneous, but multivariate time series often exhibit heterogeneous channel dependencies—different channel pairs may be governed by distinct physical laws or temporal dynamics. Standard attention struggles to capture such diversity because it uses the same token representation for all interactions.

A new generative AI model predicts 3D dose distributions for radiotherapy planning based solely on user-defined preference flavors, allowing planners to prioritize trade-offs between organs-at-risk (OARs) and planning target volumes (PTVs). In comparative evaluations, the model outperformed the Varian RapidPlan system in adaptability and plan quality in some scenarios. This approach addresses a key limitation of existing deep learning dose prediction models, which are often biased toward specific planning styles or institutional preferences due to training on reference plans. By enabling customizable trade-offs, it could lead to more personalized and efficient radiotherapy planning, potentially improving clinical outcomes and workflow. The model is designed for seamless integration with clinical treatment planning systems and generates high-quality plans efficiently. Generative AI, which can create new data samples, offers a way to incorporate user preferences directly into dose prediction.

The Cold-Start RL strategy pre-trains the model to predict verifiable clinical indices extracted from patient histories, enhancing reasoning about disease progression before determining final clinical status. ## [Study questions quantum advantage in hybrid quantum-classical neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04732) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2601.04732v2) presents a rigorous statistical study under ideal, noise-free simulation evaluating whether quantum components in hybrid quantum-classical neural networks (HQNNs) improve performance across three binary medical classification tasks. The authors find that at best hybrid models match classical counterparts, but in most cases performance deteriorates when quantum neural network (QNN) components replace matched classical layers. This empirical assessment challenges the assumption that quantum components inherently add value in hybrid models, urging cautious claims and design choices for near-term quantum machine learning applications. It is a timely reality check for the QML field, which has seen numerous proposed architectures with limited rigorous validation. The study systematically examines HQNN architectures on medical signal data and planar/volumetric images, analyzing the influence of encoding schemes, entanglement, and circuit size. Notably, the experiments are conducted under noise-free simulation, which represents an ideal upper bound and may not reflect real hardware performance.

## [Training-Free Attention-Space Contrastive Guidance Cuts LVLM Hallucinations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13707) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Attention-space Contrastive Guidance (ACG), a training-free, single-pass method that mitigates hallucinations in large vision-language models by contrasting image-conditioned and approximate text-only attention paths within self-attention layers. Experiments on CHAIR and POPE show ACG improves faithfulness over existing training-free baselines while maintaining caption quality and reducing latency by up to 2x compared to multi-pass contrastive decoding methods. Hallucinations remain a major barrier to deploying LVLMs in high-stakes applications, and ACG offers a lightweight, architecture-agnostic fix that requires no retraining or multiple forward passes. Its efficiency gains could make hallucination mitigation practical for real-time or resource-constrained vision-language systems. ACG constructs both image-conditioned and text-only attention paths in a single forward pass using a masking-based surrogate, then applies a lightweight orthogonal projection to suppress components aligned with the text-only path and reduce approximation bias. The method operates directly in self-attention layers before errors accumulate at the output layer, and it is evaluated on the CHAIR and POPE benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) extend large language models to process both images and text, but they often hallucinate—describing objects or attributes not present in the image—when language priors dominate visual evidence. Contrastive decoding mitigates this by comparing outputs from a full model and a degraded (e.g., text-only) model, but typically requires multiple forward passes, increasing latency. ACG moves this contrastive signal into the attention space within a single pass, avoiding the extra computation.

## [SpatialMem: Metric-Aligned 3D Video Memory for Language Grounding and QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14895) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SpatialMem introduces a queryable 3D memory representation built from casual egocentric RGB video that reconstructs metrically scaled indoor scenes, recognizes structural anchors (walls, doors, windows), and links open-vocabulary object nodes to 3D coordinates with visual and textual evidence. It achieves 37.5 overall on VSI-Bench (288 videos, 5,130 questions) using RGB alone, with a notable 41.4 vs. 24.4 on room-size estimation compared to a same-size frame model, and matches uniform sampling on OpenEQA's episodic-memory task at an eight-frame budget. This work shows that a structured, metric-aware 3D memory can substantially improve spatial reasoning and language grounding from ordinary video, without depth sensors or specialized hardware. It advances embodied AI and video QA by providing a compact, queryable index (14 KB per scene) that grounds every answer in linked 3D evidence, with potential impact on robotics, AR, and assistive navigation. The anchor-first hierarchy assigns retrieved objects to the correct structural parent with 0.24 accuracy on 12 ScanNet scenes versus 0.01 for open-vocabulary 3D scene graphs, though this is a capability difference rather than an overall win. The queryable index costs 14 KB per scene without linked evidence frames, and serializing memory to text underperforms a blind control on OpenEQA, highlighting the importance of evidence-frame retrieval.

**Background**: Egocentric video captured from a first-person perspective is increasingly used to teach AI systems about spatial environments, but converting casual RGB footage into metrically accurate, queryable 3D representations remains challenging. ## [OmniSpectra: A Wavelength-Native Foundation Model for Cross-Instrument Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15351) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OmniSpectra introduces a continuous-coordinate masked autoencoder that maps physical wavelengths to a logarithmic basis, turning cosmological redshift into a uniform additive shift that cancels in rotary position embeddings. Pretrained on eight surveys and benchmarked against seven baselines, it achieves state-of-the-art performance with only 12.2M parameters, outperforming substantially larger models. This work addresses a structural limitation in cross-instrument spectral representation learning—coordinate mismatch caused by token-rank positional encoding—and shows that encoding physical structure can substitute for parameter scale. It could enable more efficient and generalizable foundation models for scientific data such as astronomy spectra, benefiting researchers working with heterogeneous instruments. The model discards integer pixel indexing and uses a physics-grounded, resolution-gated Fourier decoder to counter spectral bias and provide anti-aliasing. It is pretrained on eight surveys and compared against seven baselines, with results indicating that physical structure encoding can compensate for model size.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Spectra from different instruments often have different wavelength grids, and standard sequence models encode position by token rank rather than physical coordinate, forcing destructive resampling. Masked autoencoders are self-supervised models that reconstruct masked input, and rotary position embeddings (RoPE) encode position via rotation matrices to capture relative positions. OmniSpectra combines these ideas with a logarithmic basis to handle multiplicative redshift effects natively.

## [ClKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks Extended to Clifford Algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05977) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ClKAN introduces a flexible and efficient Kolmogorov-Arnold Network architecture for function approximation in arbitrary Clifford Algebra spaces. It proposes Randomized Quasi-Monte Carlo grid generation to address exponential scaling in higher-dimensional algebras and new batch normalization strategies for variable domain inputs, validated on synthetic and physics-inspired tasks. This work bridges KANs with Clifford algebras, potentially enabling more expressive and geometrically aware neural networks for scientific machine learning and physics-informed modeling. It could benefit researchers in AI/ML and scientific computing who need to model high-dimensional algebraic structures efficiently. The use of Randomized Quasi-Monte Carlo grid generation is a practical solution to the exponential scaling of grid-based methods in higher-dimensional Clifford algebras, and the new batch normalization strategies handle variable domain inputs. However, the abstract does not provide quantitative results or comparisons with existing methods.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) are a neural network architecture inspired by the Kolmogorov-Arnold representation theorem, where learnable univariate functions replace linear weights, offering an alternative to multilayer perceptrons. Clifford algebras are associative algebras that generalize real numbers, complex numbers, and quaternions, and are widely used in geometry and theoretical physics. Randomized Quasi-Monte Carlo (RQMC) combines low-discrepancy sequences with randomization to achieve unbiased estimators and faster convergence in numerical integration.

## [StealthRL uses reinforcement learning to evade AI-text detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08934) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StealthRL, a reinforcement learning framework, trains paraphrase attacks against an ensemble of AI-text detectors, reducing the mean true positive rate at 1% false positive rate (TPR@1%FPR) to 0.024 on the MAGE test pool. The attack transfers to held-out detectors including RoBERTa, Fast-DetectGPT, Binoculars, and MAGE, indicating a broad robustness failure rather than overfitting. AI-text detectors are increasingly used in high-stakes settings such as academic integrity and content moderation, and this work shows they remain brittle under adaptive paraphrasing pressure. The findings motivate robustness evaluation at deployment-relevant operating points and raise concerns about relying solely on detectors. The attack preserves nontrivial semantic overlap and readable prose, and the paper includes detector-score analysis, repeated-generation stability checks, and automatic and judge-based quality evaluation. Code and the trained model checkpoint are publicly available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: AI-text detectors aim to distinguish machine-generated text from human-written text, often by analyzing statistical patterns or perplexity. Paraphrase attacks rewrite text to preserve meaning while altering surface features, and reinforcement learning can train a policy to adaptively generate such rewrites against detector feedback. TPR@1%FPR is a common metric that measures detection rate at a low false-positive threshold, reflecting real-world deployment constraints.

## [DAG-Based Contextual Memory Virtualisation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22402) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Contextual Memory Virtualisation (CMV), a system that models LLM session history as a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) with snapshot, branch, and trim primitives, enabling context reuse across parallel sessions. It introduces a three-pass structurally lossless trimming algorithm that preserves all user messages and assistant responses verbatim while reducing token counts by an average of 20% and up to 86% for sessions with significant overhead. This approach addresses the critical problem of context loss when LLM sessions hit context limits, potentially enabling long-running agents to maintain state across sessions without lossy compaction. It could significantly improve token efficiency and continuity for complex, multi-session tasks like coding and extended reasoning. The trimming algorithm strips mechanical bloat such as raw tool outputs, base64 images, and metadata while preserving all conversational content verbatim. A case study on 76 real-world coding sessions shows trimming remains economically viable under prompt caching, with mixed tool-use sessions averaging 39% reduction and reaching break-even within 10 turns.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models have a fixed context window, and when sessions exceed this limit, they undergo lossy compaction that discards accumulated understanding like architectural mappings and trade-off decisions. Virtual memory is an operating system technique that abstracts physical memory, allowing programs to use more memory than physically available; CMV borrows this concept to manage LLM context. A DAG is a graph with directed edges and no cycles, often used in version control systems like Git to represent branching and merging of histories.

## [MoE-SpAc Uses Speculative Decoding to Boost Edge MoE Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09983) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MoE-SpAc is a new inference framework that repurposes speculative decoding as a lookahead sensor to predict expert demand and improve memory management for Mixture-of-Experts models on heterogeneous edge devices. It integrates a Speculative Utility Estimator, a Heterogeneous Workload Balancer, and an Asynchronous Execution Engine, achieving a 42% improvement in TPS over the state-of-the-art SD-based baseline and an average 4.04x speedup over standard baselines across seven benchmarks. This work addresses the critical memory constraints of deploying large MoE models on edge devices by turning speculative decoding into a memory management tool, potentially enabling efficient on-device inference for scalable AI. The significant speedup could impact edge computing applications where latency and memory are limited. The framework uses online integer optimization for dynamic computation partitioning and unifies prefetching and eviction in the same utility space. Code is available at https://github.com/lshAlgorithm/MoE-SpAc, and experiments were conducted on seven benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models divide a neural network into specialized sub-networks (experts) and activate only a few per token, enabling scalable performance but creating memory challenges on edge devices due to dynamic expert activation. Speculative decoding is an inference-time optimization where a smaller draft model proposes multiple tokens that a larger model verifies in one pass, accelerating autoregressive generation. Expert offloading techniques prefetch and cache experts to manage memory, but existing methods struggle with I/O bottlenecks because expert activation is dynamic and low-information.

## [Strong Reward Models Can Still Be Socially Biased, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05003) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.05003v2) extends reward model evaluation to four social domains—bias, safety, morality, and ethical reasoning—and finds that strong performance on standard benchmarks does not guarantee socially aligned behavior. The analysis reveals that models may avoid some stereotypes while reinforcing others, and that stronger stereotype avoidance can trade off against contextual faithfulness. This matters because reward models are central to LLM alignment, and the findings suggest that current benchmarks poorly predict how models handle pluralistic social expectations. The work motivates more direct evaluation of the social preferences encoded in reward models, which could affect how alignment researchers and practitioners select and trust reward models. The study evaluates a broad set of reward models and shows that aggregate bias scores can hide opposing tendencies, with standard benchmark performance only inconsistently related to socially aligned behavior. The trade-off between bias mitigation and contextual faithfulness is a key caveat for those optimizing solely for lower bias scores.

**Background**: Reward models are trained to predict human preferences and are used to guide the optimization of large language models during alignment (e.g., in RLHF). Benchmarks like RewardBench evaluate these models on chat, safety, and reasoning tasks, but they typically do not capture nuanced social preferences. This paper argues that evaluating reward models on socially consequential domains is necessary to ensure they encode fair and context-sensitive preferences.

## [MoLS: SNR-Guided Module-Wise Learning-Rate Calibration for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05794) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MoLS proposes a lightweight, one-shot calibration method that estimates module-level gradient signal-to-noise ratios (SNRs) after an initial adaptation period and uses inverse-square-root scaling to set relative learning-rate multipliers, replacing costly manual tuning. The multipliers are introduced smoothly during warmup and then held fixed while the global learning-rate schedule continues. Selecting relative learning rates across functionally distinct LLM modules is a real pain point that currently requires expensive manual search, so an automatic, principled calibration can lower tuning costs and improve pre-training and fine-tuning outcomes. The method is compatible with memory-efficient optimizers and parameter-efficient fine-tuning, broadening its practical relevance. The analysis links module-wise SNR differences to structural sources and to the attenuation of Adam's normalized mean-gradient component, and theoretical results quantify calibration effects and sensitivity to estimation error and relative SNR drift. Experiments across LLM pre-training and fine-tuning show reduced perplexity and improved downstream accuracy with minimal computational overhead, matching carefully tuned module-specific learning rates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models consist of functionally distinct modules (e.g., attention and feed-forward layers) whose gradients have heterogeneous statistics. AdamW, the de facto optimizer for Transformers, adapts updates at the parameter level using first- and second-moment estimates, but it does not automatically choose relative learning rates across modules, so practitioners often hand-tune per-module multipliers. Gradient SNR, the ratio of gradient signal to noise, is a common diagnostic for how informative a gradient estimate is, and this paper uses it to derive module-wise scaling automatically.

<a id="item-200"></a>
## [Q-MMR: Off-Policy Evaluation via Recursive Reweighting and Moment Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06474) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Q-MMR introduces a recursive reweighting and moment-matching framework for off-policy evaluation in finite-horizon MDPs, learning scalar weights for each data point to approximate the target policy's expected return. Notably, it achieves a dimension-free finite-sample bound under only Q^π realizability, meaning the error does not depend on the statistical complexity of the function class. This work provides a novel theoretical framework for off-policy evaluation with a surprising dimension-free finite-sample guarantee, which could significantly impact offline reinforcement learning theory by offering new insights into coverage and function approximation. The weights are learned inductively in a top-down manner via a moment matching objective against a value-function discriminator class. The method establishes connections to existing approaches like importance sampling and linear FQE, and further theoretical analyses shed new light on the nature of coverage in offline RL.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Off-policy evaluation (OPE) aims to estimate the performance of a target policy using data collected by a different behavior policy, which is crucial for safe deployment of reinforcement learning in real-world applications. Finite-horizon MDPs are a common setting where the agent interacts for a fixed number of steps per episode. Realizability of Q^π means the true action-value function of the target policy lies within the function class used for approximation, a relatively weak assumption compared to full realizability of all policies. Moment matching is a technique to match moments of distributions, often used in function approximation and generative modeling.

**Tags**: `#off-policy evaluation`, `#offline reinforcement learning`, `#reinforcement learning theory`, `#moment matching`, `#function approximation`

<a id="item-201"></a>
## [MedAction: Tree-Structured Distillation for Active Multi-Turn Clinical Diagnosis LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07305) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MedAction introduces a tree-structured distillation pipeline that synthesizes multi-turn diagnostic trajectories via LLM-environment interaction, and uses two knowledge-graph-grounded metrics—Disease Trajectory Consistency (DTC) and Reasoning-Action Consistency (RAC)—to filter trajectory quality. The resulting dataset, MedAction-32K, contains 32,681 trajectories from 2,896 PMC cases, and fine-tuning an 8B model on it achieves state-of-the-art performance among open-source models on MedR-Bench and the curated MedAction-300-Hard benchmark. This work reframes LLM diagnosis as an active, multi-turn process that better reflects real clinical practice, and identifies specific failure modes (ungrounded test ordering, unreliable diagnostic updates, degraded multi-turn coherence) that current medical LLMs exhibit. By releasing a large distilled dataset and showing strong results with an 8B model, it pushes the edge for open-source medical LLMs and provides a template for training agents that act under evolving, partial evidence. The pipeline uses tree-structured distillation to generate diverse diagnostic trajectories, and the DTC metric tracks whether the model's hypothesis converges toward the correct diagnosis while RAC verifies that belief updates are driven by gathered evidence. The approach is evaluated on MedR-Bench and a new MedAction-300-Hard benchmark, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Most existing LLM diagnostic evaluations use static, single-turn settings where complete patient information is provided upfront, which oversimplifies real clinical practice. Active diagnosis involves starting from initial observations, ordering tests, interpreting results, and updating a differential diagnosis across multiple turns. Current medical training data teaches models to reason from complete information but not to act under evolving, partial evidence, leading to failure modes such as ungrounded test ordering and unreliable diagnostic updates.
