## [POETS: Compute-Efficient Policy Ensembles for Uncertainty-Aware LLM Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07775) ⭐️ 7.0/10

POETS introduces a framework that directly trains a policy ensemble to capture epistemic uncertainty by matching implicitly encoded reward functions to online bootstrapped data, bypassing the nested process of training an uncertainty-aware reward model and then fitting a policy. The ensemble shares a pre-trained LLM backbone while maintaining diversity through independent LoRA branches, and theoretically approximates KL-regularized Thompson sampling with optimal cumulative regret bounds of O(√(T γ_T)). This work bridges uncertainty quantification and policy optimization for LLM-based black-box optimization, offering a compute-efficient alternative to expensive LLM ensembles. It achieves state-of-the-art sample efficiency in scientific discovery domains like protein search, therapeutic molecular design, and quantum circuit design, and improves RL transfer in mathematical reasoning and code generation, potentially impacting how LLMs are fine-tuned for exploration-heavy tasks. POETS uses a shared pre-trained backbone with independent LoRA branches to overcome compute and memory constraints of ensembling LLMs, and is particularly robust in off-policy settings with experience replay or small dataset regimes. The theoretical guarantee assumes KL-regularized Thompson sampling and the regret bound depends on the effective horizon γ_T.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Thompson sampling is a heuristic for addressing the exploration-exploitation dilemma in multi-armed bandit problems by choosing actions according to a randomly drawn belief. Epistemic uncertainty refers to uncertainty due to things one could in principle know but does not, and quantifying it is crucial for efficient exploration. KL regularization is commonly used in reinforcement learning and LLM fine-tuning to keep a policy close to a reference policy, and policies trained with such regularization implicitly encode a reward function. Ensembling LLMs is a standard way to capture uncertainty but is computationally prohibitive, motivating efficient architectures like shared backbones with LoRA adapters.

## [AVCT: Theory-guided cuffless blood pressure estimation from smartphone PPG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10871) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Attractor-Vascular Coupling Theory (AVCT), a framework that derives interpretable blood pressure predictors from the geometry of photoplethysmography (PPG) attractors. Using a LightGBM estimator with leave-one-subject-out cross-validation, they report mean absolute errors (MAEs) of about 2.05/1.67 mmHg for systolic/diastolic BP in 46 ICU subjects and 1.98/0.96 mmHg in 217 ambulatory subjects. This work offers a mechanistically motivated, interpretable alternative to black-box deep learning for cuffless BP monitoring, potentially enabling convenient smartphone-based hypertension screening. If validated prospectively, it could broaden access to continuous cardiovascular monitoring in mobile health settings. The study uses two complementary datasets (BIDMC/VitalDB ICU and PPG-BP ambulatory) and shows that adding ECG-derived features yields no practically meaningful MAE improvement, while calibration mainly reduces between-subject offset error. The authors explicitly caution that the reported errors, although within commonly cited AAMI/ISO and BHS thresholds, do not establish compliance with device-validation standards.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Photoplethysmography (PPG) is an optical technique that measures blood volume changes by detecting light absorption or reflection in tissue, and it is the basis of heart-rate sensing on smartphones and wearables. Cuffless blood pressure estimation from PPG is challenging because the signal is influenced by many physiological factors; attractor reconstruction is a nonlinear dynamics method that reconstructs a system's state space from a single time series using delay coordinates. LightGBM is an open-source gradient-boosting framework for machine learning that is efficient and scalable.

## [Watermarking Should Be Treated as a Monitoring Primitive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13095) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper argues that watermarking in generative models should be evaluated and governed as a monitoring primitive, not just as a security mechanism against removal or forgery. The authors demonstrate that even zero-bit watermarks, which encode no identity, can attribute outputs to individual entities when each entity holds its own key, and that external observers without keys can identify the source of unseen text and image outputs in their tests. This reframing highlights that watermarks can link generated content to its source, creating privacy risks and raising questions about how watermarking systems are deployed and governed. It calls for evaluations to report attribution and identification accuracy alongside robustness, which could impact AI governance policies and provider practices. The attribution and identification capabilities depend on deployment choices that current evaluations do not report, specifically how fine-grained and persistent the bindings between keys and entities are, and who is allowed to query the detector. The authors recommend that governance should limit these bindings and detector access to mitigate risks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Watermarking is a technique used by generative model providers to embed a hidden signal into synthetic content, which a detector with a secret key can later recover, helping meet legal requirements to mark AI-generated content. Traditionally, watermark security is evaluated against adversaries who try to remove or forge the signal in individual outputs. Zero-bit watermarks are a type of watermark that does not encode any identity information, only indicating the presence or absence of a watermark.

## [VLMs Fail at Simple Visual Path Following Due to Visual Encoding Weakness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.15672v2) diagnoses that frontier vision-language models (VLMs) cannot robustly follow a simple continuous line, with the failure manifesting as unstable path preservation when nearby paths are present. Internal representation analysis reveals that the information needed to determine the correct path continuation degrades already at the visual encoding stage, and common remedies such as model scaling, additional reasoning, and explicit tracing instructions do not resolve the weakness. This finding is significant because it reveals a fundamental limitation in VLMs that is not fixed by scaling, suggesting that current architectures lack a robust mechanism for following continuous visual structure. It could influence future VLM design, particularly the visual encoder and how continuous spatial information is represented, and highlights a gap between strong multimodal benchmark performance and basic visual operations. The study uses controlled settings that remove confounds like crossings or overlaps to isolate the path-following ability, and finds that the same path-preservation failures and sensitivity to nearby paths persist in visually richer and more realistic settings. Model scaling provides only limited improvement, additional reasoning yields uneven gains at substantial cost, and explicit tracing instructions fail to stabilize performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are AI systems that jointly interpret images and text, typically combining a visual encoder, a vision-language connector, and a language model. They excel on multimodal benchmarks such as image captioning and visual question answering, but their ability to reliably follow continuous visual structure—like tracing a line through successive visual continuations—has remained unclear. Path following is a basic operation that tests whether a model can maintain a selected visual path without being distracted by nearby alternatives.

## [O'Prior: Synthetic Task Distributions Shape Tabular Foundation Model Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18971) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces O'Prior, a compositional realism prior for pretraining tabular foundation models, built from four coupled components: a hierarchical structural causal model (SCM) meta-generator, a modular realism engine, an explicit stress module, and a curriculum-governed, leakage-safe generation protocol. By holding architecture, optimizer, and compute fixed and varying only the synthetic task distribution, the authors show that prior design alone yields consistent and substantial gains in downstream accuracy and robustness on real tabular benchmarks. Unlike language or vision models, tabular foundation models derive almost all their inductive biases from synthetic pretraining data, yet how to design that data has been poorly understood. This work establishes synthetic prior construction as a first-order determinant of model quality, which could reshape pretraining pipelines for tabular models like TabPFN and TabICL and improve robustness in real-world deployment regimes with distributional irregularities. Ablations confirm that mechanism diversity, realism composition, and shift-aware stress each contribute independently and are not interchangeable, with gains concentrated in regimes characterized by distributional irregularities. The stress module explicitly injects confounding and support-query mismatch, and the generation protocol is curriculum-governed and leakage-safe.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Tabular foundation models are pretrained on millions of mostly simulated datasets and perform prediction via in-context learning, without task-specific training. Their inductive biases come almost entirely from these synthetic pretraining distributions, often generated using structural causal models (SCMs) that define causal relationships among variables. Standard synthetic priors tend to be too well-behaved, omitting the irregularities and failure modes that determine deployment robustness.

## [Efficient LLM Benchmarking via Feature Selection and Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25773) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper reframes efficient LLM benchmarking as multiple regression with feature selection, showing that using kernel ridge regression for prediction and mRMR for question selection substantially improves prediction accuracy and ranking correlation across benchmarks. This work demonstrates that simple, fast methods can significantly reduce the cost of evaluating LLMs while maintaining accurate rankings, which could make benchmarking more accessible and reproducible for researchers with limited compute. The approach consistently achieves smaller prediction errors (MAE and RMSE) and greater ranking correlation (Spearman ρ and Kendall τ) except in very data-poor settings, and mRMR subsampling is much faster and more stable across random seeds than competitor methods that involve probabilistic models or clustering.

**Background**: Efficient benchmarking aims to predict full benchmark scores using only a subset of questions to save computation. Kernel ridge regression is a regularized regression method that can capture nonlinear relationships, and mRMR is an information-theoretic feature selection algorithm that selects features with high relevance to the target and low redundancy among themselves. Ranking correlation metrics like Spearman ρ and Kendall τ measure how well predicted rankings match true rankings.

## [TouchSafeBench: New Benchmark Tests VLMs on Collision Grounding for Safe Human-Robot Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31196) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces TouchSafeBench, a physics-grounded benchmark built in Habitat 3.0, containing 2,940 simulated indoor co-presence episodes for evaluating whether vision-language models (VLMs) can perform collision grounding—inferring current and imminent contact between a robot and its environment or a human. It evaluates three frontier or robotics-oriented VLMs across nine visual representations on two tasks: classifying current safety state and warning about imminent collision. This work highlights a critical gap in embodied AI safety: visual fluency in VLMs does not guarantee physical accountability, as even the best model achieved an average Macro-F1 below 50%. The benchmark provides a standardized way to measure and improve collision awareness, which is essential for deploying robots in human-shared spaces. The benchmark includes synchronized multi-view RGB-D observations, top-down trajectory maps, calibrated camera metadata, and simulator-derived contact labels. Results show that explicit depth information is not automatically transformed into robot-body collision evidence, and robot-scene contact is consistently harder to detect than human-contact risk.

Habitat 3.0 is a simulator for embodied AI that models humans, avatars, and robots in realistic indoor scenes, enabling large-scale study of human-robot interaction. Collision grounding requires binding visual observations to robot body geometry, camera viewpoint, scene layout, human proximity, and temporal motion—a capability not captured by standard visual description tasks.

## [ENPO Fixes Exponential Exploration Blow-up in Iterative Nash Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01382) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.01382v3) analyzes online iterative Nash learning from human feedback (NLHF) and proves that standard iterative methods can incur an exponential dependence on the inverse KL-regularization parameter, meaning implicit exploration through policy updates is insufficient. The authors propose Exploratory Nash Preference Optimization (ENPO), which combines SFT-type regularization with adversarial policy exploration to eliminate this exponential dependence without requiring minimax oracles or explicit preference-model estimation, and they further introduce BENPO (with an O(log T) regret bound) and a practical fine-tuning variant, DENPO, which improves over RLHF and NLHF baselines on Llama-3-8B-Instruct. This work provides a learning-theoretic explanation for why simple iterative NLHF can fail and offers an algorithm that removes the exponential dependence, which matters for building alignment methods that remain efficient when human preferences are non-transitive. If the DENPO variant generalizes, it could give practitioners a more scalable alternative to reward-model-based RLHF and to NLHF methods that rely on expensive minimax oracles. ENPO replaces the problematic implicit exploration with SFT-type regularization plus adversarial policy exploration, while BENPO adds extra oracles to achieve an O(log T) regret bound; DENPO is the practical fine-tuning variant evaluated on Llama-3-8B-Instruct across multiple benchmarks. The paper is a specialized arXiv preprint with no community discussion yet, so its empirical impact remains unvalidated beyond the reported experiments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) typically trains a reward model and optimizes a policy against it, but this reward-based formulation can be restrictive when human preferences are non-transitive (e.g., A preferred to B, B to C, but C preferred to A). Nash learning from human feedback (NLHF) instead models alignment as a two-player zero-sum preference game and seeks a Nash equilibrium, avoiding an explicit reward model. KL regularization is commonly used to keep the learned policy close to a reference policy, and its strength is controlled by a KL-regularization parameter; the paper shows that iterative NLHF can scale exponentially badly with the inverse of this parameter unless explicit exploration is added.

## [Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Label Spaces and Its Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06840) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper mechanistically analyzes how reasoning-trained language models select relevant labels from tens of thousands of candidates, identifying a small, global set of attention heads that are causally necessary and sufficient for the task. It introduces MISTILL, a distillation method that supervises the student's pooled attention at decision events, nearly doubling causal recovery in a cross-family student. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and knowledge distillation, showing that understanding internal mechanisms can lead to more effective distillation for large-label-space tasks like clinical coding. It could impact how reasoning models are compressed and deployed in high-stakes domains such as healthcare. The study uses exact mean-ablation, knock-in, and null calibration to establish causal roles of attention heads on MIMIC-IV clinical coding with 5,651 candidate codes. MISTILL adds supervision of pooled attention at decision events, but a pre-registered evidence-flip test found no improvement in evidence-dependent decisions.

Knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller student, often via chain-of-thought supervision. Clinical coding involves assigning standardized ICD codes to medical records, a task with tens of thousands of possible labels.

## [CoAT: Continuous Audio Thinking for Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18273) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce Continuous Audio Thinking (CoAT), a framework that equips large audio language models with a continuous latent workspace to organize acoustic information before generating text responses, grounded by distillation from audio experts. Evaluated on Qwen2-Audio, Qwen2.5-Omni-7B, and Audio Flamingo 3, CoAT shows performance gains across audio reasoning, understanding, music classification, speech emotion, and transcription benchmarks. This work addresses a key limitation of current LALMs: their hidden states are optimized for text generation, causing rich acoustic details like prosody, pitch, and affect to be lost. By preserving this information in a continuous thinking space, CoAT could enable more nuanced audio understanding and more expressive responses, impacting applications from speech emotion recognition to music analysis. The continuous thinking block can be processed in a single prefill, so CoAT incurs no additional autoregressive decoding cost over the baseline. Further analysis confirms that the auxiliary supervision from expert distillation propagates from the thinking positions to the model's textual responses.

**Background**: Large audio language models (LALMs) extend large language models to process audio inputs, but they are typically trained to produce text-aligned outputs, which shapes their internal representations toward text generation rather than preserving acoustic richness. Knowledge distillation is a technique where a smaller student model learns from a larger teacher model's outputs or internal representations. Continuous latent reasoning, such as the Coconut approach, allows models to reason in an unrestricted continuous space instead of natural language, which can be more efficient and expressive.

## [Transformers in EHRs: Comparing Numeric Encoding Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01391) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.01391v2) systematically compares discrete, continuous, and hybrid numeric value encoding strategies for transformers on electronic health record (EHR) data, using synthetic arithmetic tasks embedded in real EHRs and clinical prediction tasks. It finds that value-concept interaction models excel on precision-sensitive tasks, while hybrid token-based approaches with binning before projection are more robust and broadly applicable, with the optimal bin count following a power-law in dataset size. This work provides practical guidance for clinical machine learning practitioners on how to encode laboratory values and other numeric features in transformer models, showing that robustness and deployability often outweigh maximal numeric precision. The empirically derived power-law for bin count offers a simple heuristic for model design, potentially impacting the reliability of EHR-based predictive models. The study reveals trade-offs between numeric precision, optimization stability, and architectural flexibility, and notes that models consistently exhibit reliable 'good enough' numeric computation rather than exact arithmetic. Clinical gains from incorporating laboratory values are task-dependent, and the optimal number of bins follows a simple power-law derived from dataset size.

Discrete encoding maps numbers to tokens (e.g., via binning), continuous encoding uses linear projections, and hybrid approaches combine both. ## [GaP: Graph-as-Policy Multi-Agent Harness for Variational Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05369) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GaP introduces a multi-agent coding harness that generates directed computation graphs from a Modular Open Robot Skill Library (MORSL) to execute variational automation tasks. It creates an internal simulation environment to rehearse task instances with different graphs in parallel, iteratively refining graph structure and parameters to improve success rates and throughput. This approach bridges interpretable robot programming with the adaptability of model-free policies, potentially closing the reliability gap for commercial and industrial automation tasks that require persistent and reliable execution. It could impact robotics developers and industries deploying robots in dynamic environments. GaP is evaluated on 8 new open VA task benchmarks (4 in simulation, 4 in real-world), showing significantly higher success rates than baselines. The system leverages TAMP and ROS-style modular skills, and the generated graphs are interpretable and executed unchanged in both simulation and real settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Variational Automation (VA) refers to tasks with larger variations in object geometry and pose than fixed automation, making them challenging for model-free policies. Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) combines high-level action sequencing with collision-free motion planning, while ROS provides a modular framework for robot software. GaP builds on these by using agentic coding to generate computation graphs as policies.

## [Semantic Anchoring Boosts Robotic Action Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13597) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.13597v3) shows that fine-tuning Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models on limited robot demonstrations erodes the semantic structure inherited from pretrained vision-language models, and proposes a plug-and-play method that anchors action representations to a semantic manifold via shared and private channels. The method yields up to +18.7% improvement on real-world in-distribution tasks and +21.5% on out-of-distribution generalization across different VLA backbones. This work addresses a critical but often overlooked issue in embodied AI: the degradation of pretrained semantic knowledge during task-specific fine-tuning, which directly impacts task success and generalization. The plug-and-play nature means it can be applied to existing VLA models without changing deployed inference, making it highly practical for improving robot learning across simulation and real-world settings. The method decomposes representations into a shared semantic channel and a private channel, both of which are discarded at inference, leaving the deployed model unchanged. Systematic probing confirms that semantic structure quality synchronizes with both task success and out-of-distribution generalization, and the approach is validated on different VLA backbones across simulation and real-world benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models combine pretrained vision-language models with action outputs to enable robots to follow instructions and manipulate objects. Fine-tuning these models on small robot datasets is standard practice, but it can cause catastrophic forgetting of the rich semantic representations learned from large-scale pretraining. The mirror neuron theory suggests that observing and executing actions share a common intention-level encoding, inspiring the idea of anchoring action representations to semantic structures.

Researchers introduced AgentGUI, a locally hosted GUI that lets users observe and steer multiple concurrent, long-running AI agents through rich trajectory visualizations and manual or automated steering. A controlled user study found a statistically significant 38% reduction in time to identify key elements in agent traces (p = 0.023), and an automated drift-prevention feature raised small local agents' task completion rate by up to 34 percentage points across a 0.8B–9B model ladder. As AI agents take on longer, more autonomous tasks, human oversight has lagged because of limited human-centered interfaces, so a tool that measurably speeds up trace inspection and prevents drift addresses a timely gap in agent observability. The findings suggest that better interfacing can meaningfully improve both human oversight efficiency and the reliability of smaller, locally run models. AgentGUI integrates with and coordinates between open-source and frontier agent frameworks, and its drift-prevention experiment used N=50 runs per model across a 0.8B–9B model ladder. The work is an arXiv preprint (2607.26300v3) with a public project website, open-source repository, and demo video, but it has not yet undergone peer review and has no community discussion.

## [FinVerse: A New Financial Time-Series Benchmark for Realistic Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03259) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FinVerse is a new financial time-series benchmark comprising 116,897 series and 171.1 million observations, designed to evaluate 43 time-series foundation models on directional accuracy, cross-sectional ranking, and portfolio backtesting. The paper reports that model rankings on FinVerse only moderately agree with those on the existing GIFT-Eval benchmark (r=0.43). This work shows that generic time-series forecasting benchmarks may not fully capture the forecast qualities that matter for financial decision-making, which could affect how practitioners select models for trading and risk management. It also provides a more informative basis for model selection by revealing substantial performance variation across financial categories and evaluation metrics. FinVerse organizes financial data according to economic semantics and evaluates target series across point-wise forecasting, cross-sectional ranking, and portfolio backtesting. The moderate correlation (r=0.43) with GIFT-Eval suggests that generic benchmarks do not fully reflect financial decision-relevant forecast quality.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) are large pretrained models adapted to forecasting tasks across many domains, and they are typically compared on broad benchmarks using uniform error metrics. GIFT-Eval is an existing general benchmark for time-series forecasting that includes 28 datasets and over 144,000 series with 177 million data points. ## [PolyMemDB: Polyglot Database for AI Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25577) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PolyMemDB is a new polyglot database system presented in an arXiv demonstration paper (arXiv:2608.25577v2) that manages heterogeneous AI agent memory types — graph, vector, probabilistic, and spatiotemporal data — in a single architecture. It includes a probabilistic inference engine combining temporal decay with semiring aggregation to resolve long-term factual conflicts and provide detailed data provenance. Long-term memory is a growing bottleneck for personal AI agents, and existing systems fragment multi-dimensional data across single-storage paradigms while lacking provenance to resolve contradictions. PolyMemDB's design directly targets this gap, potentially reducing LLM hallucinations and token overhead for personalized agent experiences. The system's probabilistic inference engine integrates temporal decay with semiring aggregation, enabling users to trace reasoning chains transparently. As a demonstration paper, it presents a concrete system design but has not yet been validated through large-scale deployment or community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Polyglot persistence means using multiple data storage technologies within a single system to meet different storage needs, as opposed to a single multi-model database. AI agents increasingly rely on long-term memory to avoid re-sending context tokens, but memory systems must handle graphs, embeddings, probabilities, and time-stamped events, and must decide which facts remain valid as they age. Temporal decay is a common technique that lowers the retrieval score of memories not recently recalled, while semirings are algebraic structures used to generalize aggregation operations in probabilistic inference.

## [KITA isolates signing keys from LLM agents via threshold signing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05901) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.05901v2) by Yu Zheng and Qizhi Zhang presents KITA, an authorization architecture that keeps the user's personal signing key and every threshold signing-key share outside all LLM processes. The system requires contributions from t distinct reviewer-signer domains before any agent action is authorized, and the authors implemented an in-process prototype using a structured-output LLM adapter and threshold BLS signatures. As autonomous LLM agents increasingly execute consequential actions like payments and permission changes, a single mistaken judgment could reach execution if the same process controls a reusable signing credential. KITA's execution-bound authorization integrity ensures each accepted authorization includes a share from an uncompromised domain, directly addressing a real emerging risk in agent security. Under threshold-signature unforgeability and the system's assumptions, an adversary controlling the proposer and fewer than t reviewer-signer domains cannot produce a valid authorization for a new action without signing contributions from t distinct domains. The prototype was validated with six system tests for quorum gating and message binding, seven controlled baseline scenarios, and cryptographic microbenchmarks measuring online signing cost and scaling behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Threshold signatures are a cryptographic primitive in which a signing key is split into shares held by multiple parties, and a valid signature requires a quorum of t shares, so no single party can sign alone. LLM agents are AI systems that can autonomously invoke tools and APIs, and when they hold signing credentials, a prompt-injected or erroneous decision can translate directly into real-world effects. KITA combines these ideas by treating reviewer approval as threshold signing, so that LLM review must be cryptographically attested by multiple independent domains before execution.

## [Training-Free Task Vectors Enable LLM Behavioral Control Without Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09054) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Training-Free Task Vectors (TFTVs), a method that computes task-vector-like directions in weight space using only forward-pass statistics, eliminating the need for fine-tuning. TFTVs map activation steering vectors to rank-one weight-space edits and support additive composition and subtraction of behaviors. This work lowers the cost of post-training model editing by avoiding fine-tuning, making behavioral control more practical for large language models. It could impact researchers and practitioners working on model editing, activation steering, and efficient adaptation of LLMs. TFTVs satisfy arithmetic properties that directly support learning via addition, forgetting via subtraction, and composition of multiple edits. Experiments show they amplify, suppress, and compose target behaviors while preserving general knowledge and problem-solving skills, outperforming other editing and steering baselines in trait control with competitive utility preservation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Task vectors are weight-space directions computed as the difference between a fine-tuned model and its pretrained initialization, enabling post-training model editing. Activation steering modifies model behavior by adding vectors to hidden activations during inference. TFTVs bridge these approaches by deriving weight-space edits from forward-pass statistics without any gradient updates.

A new arXiv preprint benchmarks vision-language models (VLMs) on synapse and neuron proofreading tasks in connectomics, evaluating 19 open and 2 closed models for synapse proofreading and 3 open and 2 closed models for neuron proofreading on the ConnectomeBench2 dataset. Most models performed at chance level zero-shot, but LoRA adaptation on a few thousand labels brought open models level with specialist models, and the best adapted VLMs even outperformed specialists on unseen species for merge-error detection. This study provides the first large-scale empirical evidence that general-purpose VLMs, when efficiently adapted, can match or exceed specialized models on a core connectomics annotation task, potentially reducing reliance on bespoke architectures and enabling cross-species transfer. It matters for computational neuroscience and AI/ML researchers building automated proofreading pipelines for large-scale electron microscopy connectome reconstruction. The evaluation covers zero-shot, four-shot in-context learning, and LoRA settings against specialist models, with cross-species transfer from fly and mouse to human and zebrafish; few-shot examples mainly helped closed and largest open models, and the project will be publicly released upon acceptance.

Vision-language models (VLMs) are multimodal models that process both images and text, and LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes pre-trained weights and injects small trainable matrices, enabling adaptation with far fewer parameters and less compute.

## [Prompting Strategies Cut Energy Use of LLM-Generated Code by Up to 25%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02571) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates 21 prompting strategies across 10 open-weight and proprietary LLMs, finding that selected prompts reduce the energy consumption of generated Python and C++ code by up to 25% and 17%, respectively, compared to a baseline prompt. At the model level, Python energy reductions reached up to 50% for Granite-4.0-H-Small, 39% for Claude 4.5 Haiku, and 28% for MiniMax M3, while C++ reductions reached up to 57% for Granite-4.0-H-Small and 6.4% for Qwen3-Coder-480B-A35B-Instruct. As AI-assisted programming becomes mainstream, the environmental impact of AI-generated software is an important consideration, yet frontier LLMs are rarely evaluated on the energy efficiency of their output. This study shows that simple prompt changes can meaningfully reduce energy use without changing the model, offering a practical lever for more sustainable software development. The study identifies 8 effective strategies from 21 candidates and tests them on both Python and C++ against a baseline prompt, but the effectiveness varies substantially across models and programming languages, with some models showing only single-digit C++ reductions. The authors emphasize that energy efficiency should be incorporated into the evaluation and optimization of LLM-based code generation.

ToolRACER is a synthetic data generation pipeline that coordinates user, assistant, and tool emulation models to produce validated multi-turn conversations, yielding ToolRACERBench, a benchmark of 5.6K trajectories across six domains and 55 personas, with about 66% containing failure-prone adversarial scenarios. Existing function-calling benchmarks mostly feature cooperative, successful interactions, so this resource fills a gap by providing adversarial trajectories for training and evaluating robust task-oriented agents; models trained on it improved end-to-end accuracy on τ²-bench and ACEBench, especially when mixed with in-domain data for small language models. ## [Interaction-as-a-Judge: Evaluating Interactive Apps via Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17895) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Interaction-as-a-Judge, an evaluation paradigm where computer-use agents (CUAs) directly interact with applications to assess them, and releases two datasets: 6,858 application pairs with human preferences and agent traces, and 222 applications with human-verified requirement labels. Experiments show improved evaluation accuracy (65.6% to 69.1% for preference prediction, 77.0% to 81.6% for requirement verification) and better generation quality compared to non-interactive methods. Current automated LLM evaluations rely on code analysis and unit tests, which can miss failures obvious to human users of interactive applications like websites and games. This work addresses that gap by using CUAs to mimic human interactive assessment, potentially improving both the evaluation and generation of interactive software by coding agents. The approach scales with interaction quality: higher-quality interactions improve performance in both preference alignment and requirement verification. At inference time, Interaction-as-a-Judge repairs 75.5% of broken requirements within five attempts (vs. 55.4% for code-only self-feedback), and as a training reward signal it improves win-rate by 6.7 percentage points while reducing code length by 35.7% and build failure rates by 7.2 percentage points compared to a code-only reward.

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that generate code for applications, and their outputs are increasingly interactive software such as websites and games. Computer-use agents are AI agents that can operate graphical user interfaces like a human, clicking and typing to interact with applications. ## [In-context neurofeedback reveals limited activation control in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20179) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces in-context neurofeedback experiments across ten open-weight models from five families (9B to 32B parameters) to test whether LLMs can control their own activation vectors. The study finds that while some models can move their activation-derived scores, only three out of ten show small but consistent control effects when sentences are prefilled, and no model learns to lower a deception monitor's score. This work addresses conflicting prior results on whether LLMs can control their activations, which is critical for AI safety because activation probes are widely used to detect misbehavior. The finding that in-context control exists in a few models, even if not yet a threat to monitors, suggests it needs to be tracked as models evolve. The experimental design separates task competence from genuine introspective access by comparing scores when sentences are generated by the subject model versus a writer model of comparable competence, and by prefilling fixed sentences to isolate activation control. The deception monitor experiment shows that one model can raise the monitor's score but none can lower it, indicating limited control over activations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Activation vectors are internal representations in neural networks, and probes are classifiers trained on these vectors to detect properties like deception or intent. Neurofeedback is a technique where humans learn to control their own brain activity by receiving real-time feedback, and recent studies have asked whether language models can similarly learn to control their activations. However, prior results were conflicting, and no design had cleanly separated whether models are just good at the task or actually accessing their own activations.

## [Agent behavior determines whether context-engineering techniques help or hurt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20959) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.20959v2) presents a large-scale empirical study of three context-engineering techniques (CETs)—semantic layers, multi-agent orchestration, and memory compaction—evaluated alone and combined across seven LLMs on 1,514 long-horizon tasks from five benchmarks. The authors identify three behavioral groups of data agents (investigators, premature answerers, and early stoppers) and show that each CET only improves accuracy for the behavior group it matches, while sometimes harming investigators. The findings challenge the assumption that context-engineering techniques are universally beneficial, showing that their effectiveness is contingent on agent behavior. This has direct implications for practitioners building data agents, who may need to diagnose agent behavior before selecting CETs, and for researchers designing adaptive context-engineering strategies. For investigators, the semantic layer stops them from looking, orchestration makes them overconfident (costing 5 percentage points), and compaction makes them repeat steps; combining CETs yields smaller gains and losses than the sum of individual effects. The study covers behavior, accuracy, and cost, and derives guidance for choosing CETs based on agent behavior, task demands, and accuracy-cost trade-offs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Data agents are LLM-based systems that answer questions by combining evidence from files, databases, documents, and the web. Context engineering refers to techniques that shape the information available to an LLM during a task: semantic layers provide structured descriptions of data, multi-agent orchestration splits work among specialized agents, and memory compaction condenses interaction history to fit within context limits. Prior work validated each technique in isolation, but how agent behavior moderates their effect was unknown.

## [MLIPS shares candidate key pools across layers to speed up sparse attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27438) ⭐️ 7.0/10

MLIPS (Multi-Layer Indexer Pool Sharing) is a training-free method that lets a producer layer publish a top-M candidate key pool, from which subsequent layers each select their own top-K keys. Implemented in vLLM for DeepSeek-V4-Flash, it achieves 1.14–1.40x speedups and 14.8–27.4% lower GPU energy across four long-context suites, with accuracy changes between -1.49 and +0.29 percentage points in single runs. Sparse attention is critical for long-context LLM inference, but finding the right keys at every layer still requires a costly full-prefix scan. MLIPS shows that sharing candidate search across layers can deliver measurable speed and energy savings without retraining, which is valuable for LLM systems engineers optimizing serving efficiency. The speedups and energy savings come with observed accuracy deltas from single runs, and more extensive sharing exposes task-dependent quality losses. The paper also introduces Top-G, an exploratory bounded pool construction for prefill, whose deployment, task accuracy, and serving benefits remain open.

MLIPS is training-free, meaning it can be applied to existing sparse-attention models without fine-tuning.

## [RLSplat: Resource-Linear Budget Control for Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27712) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RLSplat introduces a resource-linear budget-controllable feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that learns a Gaussian allocation policy to generate exactly the requested number of Gaussians, avoiding the dense prediction and pruning used by prior methods. In zero-shot evaluation, it outperforms prior budget-controllable methods at every budget, surpassing the strongest baseline even with 4× fewer Gaussians, while running up to 4.1× faster with 9.6× less peak memory at low budgets. This work addresses a key gap in budget-controllable feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting by making inference latency and peak memory scale linearly with the Gaussian budget, rather than remaining nearly constant as in pruning-based approaches. It enables practical deployment of 3D reconstruction on resource-constrained devices where both storage and compute budgets must be respected. RLSplat uses resource-linear Gaussian decoding where each Gaussian attends only to nearby Gaussians found via an efficient hierarchical neighbor search, so decoding cost decreases linearly with fewer Gaussians. The method is evaluated in a zero-shot setting, and the reported speed and memory gains are relative to prior budget-controllable methods at low budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is a rasterization-based technique for representing and rendering photorealistic 3D scenes from 2D images in real time, and it has become a popular alternative to NeRF-based methods since 2023. Feed-forward 3DGS (FF3DGS) reconstructs scenes in a single forward pass, making it suitable for limited-resource applications, and recent budget-controllable variants allow the number of Gaussians to be adjusted at inference to meet storage or latency constraints. However, these variants typically predict a dense set of Gaussians and then prune or select from it, so inference cost remains nearly constant regardless of the target budget.

## [SANE: Automated Scorer-Specific Metamorphic Testing for LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28886) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SANE is an agent-based framework that automatically derives scorer-specific metamorphic perturbation recipes and expected score relations to test the reliability of LLM judges. Across five domains, it produces 3.7 times as many validated test families as the Judge Reliability Harness (JRH) at comparable end-to-end validity, and its test-suite scores rank 11 judge models consistently with human-labeled FaithBench performance (Spearman rho = 0.852), while JRH does not (rho = 0.100). LLM judges increasingly mediate benchmark evaluation, model selection, data curation, and training, so their reliability is critical for trustworthy AI development. SANE's automated, scorer-specific approach overcomes the limitations of human-authored perturbation recipes, enabling scalable and adaptable reliability testing across diverse judges and domains. SANE encodes behavioral requirements as reusable agent instructions and includes a scorer-independent validation framework that checks whether each recipe agrees with scorer intent and whether each instantiated case realizes the recipe and supports its expected relation. It detects scorer deficiencies in every domain, including professionalism and toxicity where JRH detects none.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LLM judges are large language models used to evaluate the outputs of other models, often in place of human annotators. Metamorphic testing is a technique that checks whether a system behaves as expected when inputs are perturbed in ways that should predictably affect outputs, without needing a ground-truth oracle. Existing LLM-judge testing harnesses like JRH rely on human-authored perturbation recipes, which limits their scalability and adaptability to new scorer types.

## [Spiking Ternary GRU with Resonate-and-Fire Neurons Achieves Single-Digit WER on LibriSpeech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29720) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Spiking Ternary GRU (stGRU), which couples ternary-weight gated recurrent units with Resonate-and-Fire (RF) neurons to enable native binary spike transmission for continuous speech recognition. Trained end-to-end on LibriSpeech-960, the Small, Medium, and Large stGRUs achieve 8.09%, 7.04%, and 6.44±0.17% WER on test-clean without an external language model. This is the first matrix-MAC-free spiking network without a learned ANN sequence decoder to achieve single-digit WER on LibriSpeech, showing that SNNs can be competitive on continuous, context-dependent tasks like ASR while retaining energy-efficient event-driven computation. It could significantly advance energy-efficient speech recognition and neuromorphic computing. The architecture makes binary event transmission a native training constraint rather than a separate deployment-time quantization step, and together with ternary weights, learned projections avoid dense floating-point matrix MACs. The trained networks develop a depth-graded hierarchy: shallow layers act as fast acoustic filters, while deep layers form slower semantic integrators whose latent representations segregate into part-of-speech manifolds and word-level spike events, with deep-layer firing rates following lognormal distributions with a sparsity gradient.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) are brain-inspired models that process information via sparse, event-driven spikes, promising low-energy inference. However, the commonly used Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neuron suffers from membrane decay that erases information across pauses and phonetic boundaries, limiting performance on continuous tasks like automatic speech recognition (ASR). Resonate-and-Fire (RF) neurons, inspired by cortical processing, can better capture oscillatory dynamics and retain information over longer timescales. Ternary weights use three discrete values (-1, 0, 1) to reduce memory and computation, and when combined with binary spikes, they enable multiplication-free operations.

## [GeoDiv KAN: Geometry-Gated Divergence-Free Network for Fractional Navier-Stokes on Complex Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30179) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces GeoDiv KAN, a geometry-gated, divergence-free Gaussian radial basis function Kolmogorov-Arnold network that solves time-fractional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations on square, circular, and star-shaped domains. It reports higher predictive accuracy than PINN, PIKAN, RBF KAN, and DF RBFKAN baselines, and is further applied to two data-driven CFD cases: unsteady flow past a circular cylinder and turbulent RANS flow through a U-bend. This work advances scientific machine learning for fractional PDEs on complex geometries, a setting where standard PINNs struggle due to temporal nonlocality, nonlinearity, and incompressibility constraints. By enforcing incompressibility by construction and demonstrating realistic CFD reconstruction, it could benefit researchers in computational fluid dynamics and engineering modeling who need accurate surrogates for non-Newtonian or anomalous transport flows. The architecture combines geometry-dependent feature gating with a streamfunction formulation to preserve incompressibility by construction, and uses Gaussian radial basis functions within the KAN framework. Evaluation covers three synthetic domain shapes and two benchmark datasets (Zenodo cylinder flow and Hugging Face U-bend CFD), though the abstract does not report quantitative error metrics or computational cost comparisons.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) embed governing partial differential equations into the training loss, allowing neural networks to solve forward and inverse problems without labeled data. Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (KANs) are an alternative to multilayer perceptrons that replace fixed activation functions and linear weights with learnable univariate functions, often parameterized by splines. Time-fractional Navier-Stokes equations generalize classical fluid dynamics by introducing a fractional time derivative that captures memory and nonlocal effects, making them harder to solve numerically, especially on irregular domains.

## [Atomic Interpretable Unit Framework Reveals Hidden Polysemanticity in SAEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30474) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an atomic interpretable unit framework for evaluating sparse autoencoders (SAEs), formalizing the ultimate objective of SAE evaluation as requiring features to form a coordinate system for semantically meaningful explanation. It also organizes existing and new measurements such as DAME (Dominance-Aware Monosemanticity Evaluation) as proxy metrics and presents a comprehensive benchmarking framework that evaluates hundreds of SAEs across multiple architectures and model families. This work addresses a gap in interpretability research by providing a formal objective for SAE evaluation, which could influence future SAE design and training. It uncovers failure modes where SAEs appear monosemantic under existing metrics but remain functionally polysemantic, highlighting the need for dominance-aware evaluation before deploying SAE features for attribution, circuit tracing, or intervention. The framework evaluates hundreds of SAEs and finds that the most dominant features—those contributing most to model computation—are systematically less interpretable and more redundant. This suggests that current evaluation metrics may be ad hoc and that dominance-aware checks are necessary as a standard practice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a technique for decomposing neural network activations into sparse, interpretable features, motivated by the goal of discovering monosemantic features—individual features that correspond to a single concept. Polysemanticity, where a single neuron responds to multiple unrelated concepts, is a key challenge in interpretability. As SAEs are increasingly used for tasks like attribution and circuit discovery, researchers have adopted various metrics (e.g., sparsity, reconstruction fidelity) to evaluate the global properties of the learned feature set, but these metrics lack a unifying objective.

## [VANE: Next-Frame Prediction in Embedding Space for Scalable Visual World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30724) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VANE introduces a self-supervised visual pretraining framework that predicts the representation of an entire next frame in a fixed embedding space provided by a frozen semantic tokenizer, using a causal encoder and state predictor. It also unifies image and video modeling by constructing local-to-global crop sequences for still images, and achieves state-of-the-art results on most anticipation, recognition, and dense perception benchmarks among unified encoders, surpassing V-JEPA 2.1 by 7.1 points in mean-class recall@5 on EPIC-KITCHENS-100 action anticipation. This work addresses a real gap between masked video pretraining, which recovers missing content from context, and autoregressive methods, which predict token-by-token but lag in representation quality. By showing that next-frame prediction in embedding space scales with model and data size, it offers a promising path toward unified, scalable visual world models that could benefit video understanding, robotics, and embodied AI. Prediction occurs in a fixed embedding space from a frozen semantic tokenizer to emphasize scene content and structure, while a lightweight auxiliary generative decoder reconstructs future-frame patches conditioned on the predicted representations to retain fine-grained spatial detail. The method is evaluated as an arXiv preprint (v2) and demonstrates scaling behavior, but no community discussion or peer review is yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Visual pretraining methods often use masked modeling, where parts of an image or video are hidden and the model learns by reconstructing them, or autoregressive modeling, which predicts future tokens sequentially. Joint-embedding methods instead learn representations by aligning different views in a shared embedding space and have shown strong representation quality. World models aim to learn predictive representations of the environment, and video provides a natural signal: what happens next. VANE combines these ideas by predicting the next frame's embedding rather than raw pixels or discrete tokens.

## [OpenAttention: One Percent Is Enough with Tail Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30951) ⭐️ 7.0/10

OpenAttention proposes replacing tail sampling in sparse attention with a count-aware mean proxy, showing lower output error at very low read rates (≤5%) and backing the claim with closed-form weight-error analysis. On RULER-32K HARD with Llama-3.1-8B, it retains 99% of dense accuracy at a 1% read and runs decode attention 10× faster than dense attention with a custom kernel. This work challenges the common practice of sampling the unread tail in sparse attention and offers a training-free pipeline that could significantly reduce KV-cache reads during LLM inference. If validated, it could enable longer context and cheaper decoding for large language models without retraining. The method combines Query-Geometry Clustering (QGC), an ErrorGate that allocates reads by estimated weight error per cluster, and top-k selection followed by a count-aware mean proxy. The analysis shows that at low reads, sampling nearly doubles the weight error of discarding, while a single proxy stays below it; however, results are from an arXiv preprint without peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Sparse attention speeds up LLM inference by reading only a small subset of the key-value (KV) cache, which stores past token representations. A key challenge is how to handle the unread tail: discarding it biases the output, while sampling it can miss important tokens (heavy hitters) at very low read rates. OpenAttention addresses this by approximating the tail with a count-aware mean proxy and using clustering and error-aware selection to choose which parts of the cache to read.

## [Open-book scores for LoRA adapters mostly measure generic reading, not document knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31017) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper trains 150 single-document LoRA adapters on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and finds that open-book scores mostly reflect a shared reading behavior: a random adapter recovers 82% of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model, but only 2% on a cloze probe without the source text. This challenges the assumption that open-book evaluation measures document-specific knowledge in LoRA adapters, which has implications for how researchers evaluate and select adapters for knowledge merging and retrieval. Specialists beat the base model on cloze recall by 0.094, and their weight updates follow document geometry, but adapters selected by weight-space proximity or text embedding were not distinguishable from random ones on either probe in a 30-query test; correctly scaled merges of 16+ documents recovered 87-89% of open-book advantage but only 12-16% of cloze advantage, and the authors report four silent errors that produced clean but wrong results.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that adapts large language models by training small low-rank matrices instead of all parameters. Single-document LoRA adapters are trained on individual documents and proposed as modular units of knowledge that can be merged or retrieved. Open-book scoring evaluates a model with the source document included in the prompt, while cloze probes test recall of facts without providing the source text.

## [SABLE: Scalable Bayesian Optimization for High-Dimensional Latent Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31495) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SABLE introduces a Bayesian optimization method for high-dimensional latent spaces with isotropic distributions and limited evaluation budgets, using an affine surrogate that decomposes each query into an in-span component optimized by expected improvement (EI) in at most N dimensions and an off-span component governed by a scalar norm. The method prescribes the off-span norm via a finite-horizon schedule to avoid underexploration, and a recompression scheme reduces information to O(M) pseudo-observations, bounding refitting cost by O(M^2D + M^3) while keeping decisions to tens of milliseconds. This work addresses a practical bottleneck in optimizing expensive-to-evaluate designs (e.g., engineering designs, proteins) where generative models provide latent spaces with thousands to tens of thousands of dimensions, far exceeding the evaluation budget. By enabling sample-efficient optimization in such regimes with orders-of-magnitude faster decision times, SABLE could significantly impact generative design and Bayesian optimization workflows. SABLE treats higher-order structure as surrogate discrepancy and shows that the off-span norm implicitly selected by affine EI can lead to underexploration; instead, it prescribes the norm using a finite-horizon schedule without restricting search to a learned subspace. The recompression scheme bounds refitting cost to O(M^2D + M^3) and keeps parameter inference, prediction, and acquisition decisions to tens of milliseconds, with experiments across synthetic benchmarks and latent-space optimization tasks demonstrating high sample efficiency.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient approach for optimizing black-box functions, commonly using a surrogate model (e.g., Gaussian process) and an acquisition function such as Expected Improvement (EI) to balance exploration and exploitation. Generative models like VAEs or GANs provide continuous latent representations, but their latent spaces can have thousands to tens of thousands of dimensions, making standard BO impractical when the evaluation budget is small. SABLE builds on affine surrogate modeling and decomposes queries into in-span and off-span components to handle this high-dimensional, low-budget regime.

## [CEMIR vs. FISER: No Significant Difference in Pragmatic Intent Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32022) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.32022) presents a comparative study benchmarking FISER, a structured social and embodied reasoning framework, against CEMIR, a context-enriched multimodal intent recognition method that synthesizes narratives and photorealistic images from text prompts. Evaluating both approaches across four LLMs (from Qwen2.5-Omni-3B to GPT-5) on the HandMeThat v2 benchmark, the authors find no statistically significant performance difference across any model or difficulty level, even in an ablation isolating CEMIR's narrative and visual components. This study identifies a boundary condition for text-derived multimodal enrichment: when task constraints are already present in the textual instruction, synthetic multimodal augmentation may not outperform structured reasoning on the same text. The finding can guide researchers and practitioners in human-robot interaction to avoid unnecessary multimodal data generation and instead focus on backbone model capability and ambiguity difficulty. The analysis uses multiple-comparison correction and covers sixteen model-by-difficulty comparisons, plus a targeted ablation of CEMIR's narrative and visual components. Performance is primarily associated with backbone capability and the inherent difficulty of the ambiguity, not with the enrichment approach.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Pragmatic intent recognition in human-robot interaction involves inferring a speaker's underlying goals from ambiguous natural language instructions, which requires understanding unstated intent rather than just missing scene information. Two paradigms have emerged: structured symbolic reasoning (e.g., FISER) and multimodal input enrichment (e.g., CEMIR), the latter generating synthetic narratives and images to supplement text. The HandMeThat v2 benchmark provides a testbed for such pragmatic ambiguities, and the study evaluates models ranging from small (3B parameters) to large (GPT-5) to assess generality.

## [Learned Nonlinear Waveform Fusion Boosts ISAC Localization and Image Transmission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32099) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.32099) proposes a learned nonlinear waveform fusion method that combines image features with a linearly frequency-modulated (LFM) reference across scales for integrated sensing and communication (ISAC). On 512 test images, the proposed waveforms reduced normalized localization error by 29.0% and 27.0% compared to time-domain superposition and learned spectral linear fusion, respectively, while improving mean PSNR by 1.40 dB and 0.82 dB. This work addresses a core challenge in ISAC: how to jointly optimize communication and sensing without the constraints of fixed superposition or frequency partitioning. If validated, the approach could enable more efficient joint radar-communication systems for 6G networks, benefiting researchers and engineers working on waveform design for dual-function systems. The method uses adaptive temporal feature weighting and spectral updates to generate a waveform with fixed total energy over the array frame, and employs a neural demixer at the user equipment conditioned on the reference and channel state information. At the base station, a coherent dictionary is defined by the transmit pulse for sequential localization with learned local correction, which alone reduces mean normalized localization error by 12.2% relative to the same receiver without learned correction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) is a key 6G concept that uses the same radio signals for both data transmission and environmental sensing, such as detecting and localizing objects. Traditional ISAC waveform design often relies on fixed superposition of communication and sensing signals or frequency partitioning, which limits flexibility. Linear frequency-modulated (LFM) waveforms are widely used in radar for their good range resolution and Doppler sensitivity, and here they serve as a deterministic reference for sensing.

## [ELBP: A Four-Layer SLM Architecture for On-Device Behavioral Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32193) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Evidence-Grounded Local Behavioral Prediction (ELBP), a four-layer architecture that uses small language models (SLMs) to predict future user assistance needs on-device. The best configuration, Qwen2.5-3B with ELBP, achieved an F1-score of 0.80 and PR-AUC of 0.84, a 15.9% relative improvement over raw-event prompting and 17.6% over XGBoost, while reducing expected calibration error (ECE) by 43.2%. This work shows that compact SLMs, when combined with structured behavioral representations and evidence verification, can outperform both raw prompting and traditional ML models for privacy-sensitive on-device prediction. It could enable proactive assistance in high-stakes environments without sending personal interaction data to the cloud, addressing computational and data-sovereignty constraints. The architecture combines deterministic interaction processing, dual-view behavioral representation, SLM-based prediction, and evidence verification with abstention. With evidence verification and abstention, selective F1 increased to 0.84 at 91% coverage, and evidence support reached 96%, though the abstract is truncated and full technical details are not yet available.

Behavioral prediction involves analyzing sequences of user interactions to anticipate future needs, and evidence verification with abstention allows a model to refrain from predicting when confidence is low, improving reliability.

## [PEAR: Adaptive Advantage Reshaping for Perception and Exploration in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32216) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces PEAR, an adaptive advantage reshaping method that retains visual dependency as the base signal and adds an entropy-based exploration gain only at high-entropy positions, with lower visual dependency yielding a larger gain. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B show PEAR achieves the highest average performance among evaluated baselines across multimodal reasoning benchmarks, alongside leading results on textual reasoning and general multimodal benchmarks. This work addresses a specific limitation of prior methods that scale token entropy by visual dependency, which can underweight high-entropy tokens with low visual dependency despite their importance for reasoning exploration. It offers a more balanced approach to improving both perception and exploration in large vision-language models, potentially benefiting multimodal reasoning tasks. PEAR fuses visual dependency and entropy-based exploration gain into positive token-level weights that reshape the sequence-level advantage, ensuring high-entropy tokens with low visual dependency receive larger additional gains. The method is evaluated on Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B models, but the abstract does not specify the benchmarks used or the magnitude of improvements.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) combine visual and textual information for multimodal reasoning. Reinforcement learning with advantage reshaping is a technique used to guide model exploration by modifying the advantage function, often incorporating token entropy to encourage diverse reasoning paths. Visual dependency measures how much a model relies on visual input versus language priors, and prior work has used it to scale entropy-based exploration.

## [OVC: Outcome-Conditioned Visual Credit for Multimodal RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32324) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Outcome-Conditioned Visual Credit (OVC), a plug-in framework that separates visual-association measurement from token-level credit assignment in multimodal reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards. Instantiated with GSPO-token, OVC improves six-benchmark mean accuracy by 1.86–2.39 percentage points across three models. Uniform response-level advantage assignment ignores the distinct roles of visual observations and reasoning tokens, so OVC's finer-grained credit could make multimodal RLVR training more effective without extra reward models or annotations. This matters for teams training vision-language models on reasoning tasks with verifiable answers. OVC uses Relative Visual Encoding to contrast each predictive state's affinity to image versus prompt-context representations, then calibrates this evidence via outcome-conditioned mean contrasts scaled by within-side variation, and finally produces detached positive token weights with unit global mean that preserve advantage signs. It reuses existing rollouts without auxiliary reward models, process annotations, or inference-time modules, and retains GSPO's sequence-level clipping.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains models using objective, programmatically checkable rewards, and has proven effective for math and coding reasoning in large language models. In multimodal settings, a single response-level advantage is typically broadcast to every token, which conflates the contribution of visual observations with that of subsequent reasoning steps. Credit assignment—deciding which tokens deserve more or less update—is therefore a key open problem for extending RLVR to vision-language models.

This work shows that state space models like Mamba-2 can enable extremely lightweight, low-latency EEG classification suitable for continuous monitoring in high-risk environments, potentially advancing adaptive safety systems and brain-computer interfaces. ## [PILOT: Latent Reasoning Exploration for Multimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32474) ⭐️ 6.0/10

PILOT introduces a framework that models reasoning as a latent variable with distinct prior and posterior reasoning modes in a shared MLLM, using an importance-weighted variational objective and prior-posterior co-evolution to explore retrieval-relevant reasoning traces. On the 78-task MMEB-V2 benchmark, it outperforms the primary baseline across all 12 task groups at both 2B and 7B parameters, improving overall scores by 3.2 and 3.3 points, with the largest gains in visual-document retrieval (+6.1 and +6.4 points). This work reframes relational supervision as a signal for shaping reasoning exploration rather than a post-hoc reward, which could improve how reasoning-enhanced embedding models are trained for multimodal retrieval. The consistent gains across all task groups at two model scales suggest the approach is broadly applicable to retrieval systems that rely on MLLM-generated reasoning. The prior mode conditions only on the input, while the posterior uses additional training-time relational evidence to guide exploration; posterior trajectories are selectively transferred to the prior based on utility and prior reachability. The method is evaluated at 2B and 7B parameters on the 78-task MMEB-V2 benchmark, with the largest improvements in visual-document retrieval.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal embeddings map inputs such as images and text into a shared vector space for tasks like retrieval, and recent methods use Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to generate self-reasoning that enriches the embedded input. However, standard retrieval supervision only specifies pairwise relevance without guiding which reasoning traces are useful, and since reasoning is not directly observed, multiple plausible traces can introduce varying retrieval utility. Latent variable modeling treats such unobserved reasoning as a hidden variable with prior and posterior distributions, a common technique in variational methods.

## [Radio Foundation Models Enriched with Channel Configuration Metadata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32542) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.32542) proposes incorporating channel configuration (CFG) information into Radio Foundation Model (RFM) pre-training, producing CFG-enriched embeddings that serve as inductive priors. Experiments show these embeddings outperform or match raw channel tokens and baselines on LoS/NLoS classification, timing advance estimation, and beam selection, especially under label scarcity and unseen configurations. This work shows that injecting domain-specific configuration metadata into self-supervised pre-training can improve generalization of radio foundation models under distribution shift and limited labels, which is important for real-world wireless deployment where labeled data is scarce and channel conditions vary. It suggests a practical path to more robust and data-efficient RFMs for 5G/6G networks. The evaluation covers three data regimes and both in-distribution and out-of-distribution configuration settings, comparing CFG-enriched embeddings against raw channel tokens and multiple baselines. Gains are consistent but incremental, and the approach assumes channel configuration metadata is available during pre-training and inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Radio Foundation Models (RFMs) are large pre-trained models for wireless signals, analogous to foundation models in vision and language, designed to learn general representations from channel data that transfer to tasks like beam selection and positioning. Channel configuration refers to parameters such as antenna settings, bandwidth, and numerology that shape how signals are transmitted and received. Incorporating such metadata as an inductive prior helps the model adapt to different network configurations without retraining from scratch.

## [LLaCO: Continuous Diffusion Language Model with Competition-Aware Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32604) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Competition Threshold Theory (CTT) to explain temporal compression in continuous diffusion language models and proposes Semantic Retention Scheduling (SRS) to align time allocation with identity decay. It presents LLaCO, an 8B generative framework that uses a spherical noising process and embedding directional adapter, outperforming previous continuous diffusion baselines and rivaling discrete diffusion models of comparable scale. This work addresses a key limitation in continuous diffusion language models, potentially advancing non-autoregressive text generation by improving the alignment between geometric and temporal progression. If validated, it could lead to more efficient and higher-quality diffusion-based language models, impacting researchers in diffusion models and NLP. The paper shows that embedding norm decay causes Euclidean distance to yield spurious overtaking, and that high-dimensional overtaking requires extreme noise levels, concentrating identity decay near the noisy endpoint. LLaCO employs a spherical noising process formulated as geodesic interpolation toward a mask-biased endpoint to eliminate norm decay while preserving token competition.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Continuous diffusion language models are a non-autoregressive approach to text generation that operate in continuous embedding spaces, where tokens are represented as vectors and noise is gradually added and removed. Unlike autoregressive models that generate text left-to-right, diffusion models iteratively denoise a sequence, potentially enabling parallel generation. Temporal compression refers to the misalignment between the geometric trajectory of embeddings and the temporal progression of denoising, which can degrade generation quality.

**Tags**: `#diffusion-models`, `#language-models`, `#text-generation`, `#non-autoregressive`, `#machine-learning`

## [WiMoT Enables Replay-Free Continual Wi-Fi Sensing via Moment Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32660) ⭐️ 6.0/10

WiMoT introduces a replay-free continual learning method for Wi-Fi sensing that stores per-class feature mean and covariance instead of raw CSI samples, and transports these statistics via an affine map on a frozen feature basis when the representation changes. On three datasets (3DO, WiMANS, Widar3.0), it achieves final accuracies of 94.02%, 89.61%, and 95.16% with at most 0.58 percentage points of forgetting. This approach addresses the privacy and storage constraints that prevent Wi-Fi sensing nodes from retaining historical CSI data, enabling continual learning across new rooms or users without storing raw samples. It outperforms 21 baselines, including replay-based methods, on two datasets and matches the strongest replay baseline on the third while eliminating forgetting, which could make edge-deployed Wi-Fi sensing systems more practical and privacy-preserving. WiMoT uses supervised contrastive learning for new domains, a closed-form loss on stored statistics to keep earlier classes separable, and a Gaussian discriminant classifier refitted from transported statistics with equal weight per domain, requiring no domain identity at inference. Storing full covariances as rank-32 factors reduces moment memory to 6.4% with at most 0.49 pp accuracy loss, fitting edge-node storage budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 26, 04:00

**Background**: Wi-Fi sensing leverages Channel State Information (CSI) from Wi-Fi transmissions to detect human activities and environmental changes without requiring devices on users. Continual learning aims to learn new tasks without forgetting old ones, but in Wi-Fi sensing, nodes often cannot retain CSI data due to privacy and storage constraints, making replay-based methods infeasible. WiMoT addresses this by storing only feature statistics (mean and covariance) and updating them via an affine transformation when the feature representation changes.
